AGENT:

JOSEPH S. GARLAND, 511 53 & 55, BRIDGE STREET, WARRINGTON. Phone No. 79.

During the past year Liberalism in Warrington had gone forward. He was not taking any credit for that— he believed the credit lay in the commonsense of the workingmen and women who were beginning to see things through clear glasses, the smoke having worn off. The Liberal party had been working assiduously to educate the masses on the questions of the hour. They were the supporters of the strongest and most courageous Government in modern history. (Applau3e.) The work of the present administration was such as to fill every progressive with pride. (Hear, hear). Liberals valued themselves for one thing above all others—and that was sturdy independence of character and freedom of thought. They could tell the illustrious 1 people who were constantly twitting them on their efforts, to go ahead with their dumping exhibitions, bugle bands and waving of fligs, which, if their origin could be as,-ertained, probably came from the same place as the clocks. (Laughter). They were not daunted by the tactics of the Tory party. They had before them a set purpose— to brighten the conditions of the working classes, and especially of the children. Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., with all the effrontery of a backwoodsman, dared to • come into the limelight and demand a return from the Liberal Government of appointments made during the past six years—with a veiled insinuation that the Government were a corrupt Government and had been creating jobs for their friends. If he asked for a return for the plst twelve years he would -probably get more than he expected. (Hear, hear).

===== 0002601_19120224_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. SMITH- HAD BITTEN 'OFF MORE THAN I{E COULD

CHEW in bringing forward such a motion, and he (the speaker) made hold to say that when the return was made Mr. Smith's political reputation would be irretrievably blasted—as Mr. Bonar Law's had teen by recent events. Referring to the promised abolition of the plural vote, Mr. New said the vote of a workingman ought to count for just as much as the vote of a lord or any man of high standing. (Applause) Why should a man have greater political power because he happened to have been born in more advantageous surroundings or because he had accumulated riches in questionable ways ? With regard to the National Insurance Act, it did not mean that a min could not belong to more than one society. ir, however, he uas a member of several he must declare for one which should become an approved society and draw his benefits through it. He could belong to six or seven clubs and draw as much as £6 or .£7 a week if he cared to pay the additional contributions. The Act, would work

===== 0002601_19120224_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
A WONDERFUL REVOLUTION

in our modern civilization. From whatever point of view they regarded that great and beneficent measure, no member of a friendly society or trade union could fail to have feelings of satisfaction with the lasting benefits it would confer. Mr. New laid special stress upon the liability of the forge worker to attacks of disease,through exposure and chills,and pointed out that by means of asnatoria, which would be available for all insured workers, a great effort would be made to stamp out consumption. Workingmen. continued Mr. New, would have nothing to gain but everything to lose by a policy of taxing food and making all coruodi- ties dearer. They stood to gain incalculably by main- taining open markets and sending our goods to every quarter of the globe. Again alluding to Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., the speaker said that a man who would help to howl down the Prime Minister in Parliament and then come to Warrington and pose as a censor of everybody and everything ought DOG to be entrusted with the representation of the borough. Mr. Crosfield always played the game fairly and never stooped to personalities. He stood for principles that were dear to their hearts. Whenever Liberalism ceased to extend freedom or to espouse the gospel of progress for the class to which he belonged he (Mr. New) would cease to be a Liberal. When the National Insurance Act was introduced,Mr. F. E. Smith accepted it and said that no criticisms of his would ever wreck such a beneficent measure for the working classes. Since then he, along with other members of the Opbosition, had toed the line. There was

NO INDEPENDENCE IN THE TORY PARTY. If orders were not obeyed ,the recalcitrant ones went the way of I.ord ffugh Cecil, Lord Balfour of Burleigh and the Duke of Devonshire, and had to go out into the cold and Wong° their lonely furrow. Alluding to Home Rule, Mr. New said that although Mr. Toole was a son of Erin he was no firmer supporter of Home Rule than he (Mr. New.) He bad always been taught to stand by the weak and by those who wanted protection. It was because he realized that Ireland had suffered so long from the bad and careless treat- ment of this country, that he was delighted to know that Home Rule was so near accomplishment. He did not say the Bill would be on the Statute Book within twelve months—be knew it would not. The House of Lords would no doubt throw it out for three sessions but at the end of that time it would become law. It would only be a case of

===== 0002601_19120224_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
PROLONGING THE AGONY

for every year thousands of Irishmen were emigrating and the exodus could only be stayed by improved conditions of government. Surely if we could grant self-government to Austral ia,Canada and the greatest enemy we had had in modern times—South Africa—and turn a race of foes into peaceful friends and loyal adherents, it was not too much to expect that the conferring of the same rights upon Ireland would have equally happy results. (Applause). On tne motion of Mr. J. HEALEY, seconded by Mr. P. H. BROWN, a hearty vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman.

During the evening an excellent entertainment was given. Mr. Val Housden delighted the assembly with a dramatic recital, and Mr. Batten's songs proved highly popular. Mr. Sam Aukland gave concertina solos in a very skilful and pleasing style, and Mr. E. A. Sanders was warmly applauded tor a Lancashire dialect recita- tion. Mr. T. F. Wallington was an able accompanist.

POISONED SORES FROM A NAIL ------ CLEANSED & HEALED BY ZAM-BUK.

CITE.II' OINTMENTS QUITE USELESS.

Only Zam-Buk's wonderful antiseptic healing could rid Mr. Chas. F. Wakeford of a skin trouble which threatened to poison his whole body. The experience of Mr. vVakeford, who lives at 4, Evelyn Villas, Devon- shire Road, Merton, Surrey, proves once more the folly of depending upon cheap ointments for a cure. "I scratched the back of my right hand with a nail while opening a window," he told a local reporter. "A few days later the scratch started festering and an ugly ulcer developed. To my dismay, three other ulcers broke out, two on the right and one on the left hand. As cheap ointments and salves failed, I saw a doctor, who said there were nasty disease germs in the ulcers and my flesh was badly poisoned. "Lancing and poulticing by the doctor were tried without success, and as other ulcers now broke out on my arm and leg, I went to St. John's Skin Hospital, London. No good came of their treatment, however, so in despair I returned to common ointments. But I soon realised the folly of expecting any good from cheap preparations, which only set up great irritation and soreness.

"It occurred to me that Zam-Buk which people spoke so well about might do good, so I tried this new balm. It was early evident that Zam-Buk was killing thegerni-life and drawing out the poison. At the same time my tortured skin was soothed and the inflammation and swelling went down. "Zam-Buk thoroughly cleansed and purified all the poisoned sores before closing them up and healing them with new healthy skin." There is no healer like Zam-Buk, which derives its unique properties from certain rare herbs and a costly and secret process of manufacture. Theie's nothing like Zarn-Buk in action and effect. Note that Zam- Buk is not sold in penny boxes, but only in sealed packages in three sizes, viz., Is. Id., 2s. 9d. and 43. fid. All imitations are worthless.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. There was a good meeting of the Wycliffe Society on Sunday. Mr. Farrar presided and read the new President's address to the Brotherhoods in the federation. The Rev. H. Williamson gave the address upon "Burden bearing." Mr. McArthur sang "The Lord is love," and "When I survey the wondrous cross " in a very pleasing manner. Mr. Payton presided at the organ. Mr. NlcEachern and Mr.Healey also took part in the meeting. The President in his address described the P.S.A. movement as one" which was not simply national but was becoming more and more international, and which, he believed, was destined to become universal. These societies were not simply centres of entertainment and pleasant hours but centres of light and gracious influence by which the world was to be blessed.

THE COST OF EDUCATION.

ARE WE GETTING VALUE FOR MONEY?

LARGE INCREASE IN LOCAL ESTIMATES.

Are we getting value for the heavy expenditure of public money on education ? This question was raised at the monthly meeting of the War! ingtou Education Committee on Monday evening when estimates involv- ing an increase La the call on the rates to the extent. of £3,soo—whico, we understand, is equal to about 3d in the —were submitted for approval. Summarised, the estimates are as follows : Committee. Expenditure. Receipts. Deficiency. s. d. s. U. s. d. School Attendance 2,018 0 0 200 0 0 2,118 0 0 School Management 41,314 0 0 30,671 0 0 10,643 0 0 Sites and Buildings 670 0 0 20 0 0 640 0 0 Medical Inspection 710 0 0 710 0 0 Finance

55,727 17 0 31,156 0 0 24,571 17 0 Total estimated deficiency (1912-1913) do. do. (1911-12) Amount to be provided from the Borough rate for educational purposes during the year 191f-1913 £'24,000 0 0 (1911-12 5:20,500 0 0

Increase Councillor H Roberts presided at the meeting and there eere also present : Aldermen A Bennett, James Evans and J Smethurst ; Councillors J Wilkinson, C Jackson Holmes, It Henshall, W J Wilson, J Pemberton, J Dickenson, W H Haitian, T Birtles, and 0 Toole ; Revs Canon Morley Stevenson, J Davies and Neville Wilson, Mrs C Jackson Holmes. Messrs A J Jullev, C S Burgess, C E Parker, G Walker, E Plinston, 11 Kingdom J Upson, it T Fairclough, J Moore Murray (director), and R t Roberts (assistant director). Mr. A. J. JOLLEY, as chairman of the Finance Com- mittee, submitted the " Budget" statement. He pointed out that the estimates, as in previous years, were drawn up on thoroughly sound lines. There had been no attempt to under-estimate the expenditure or to over-estimate the revenue. In this connection Mr. Jolley warmly complimented the Director of k.:414p., tion and the Borough Treasurer, to whom great was due for the fact that the actual expenditure each year since the committee began its work had agreed so closely with the amount set down in the estimates. Turning to the outstanding features of the School Attendance Committee's estimates, Mr. Jolley pointed out that the only item in which there was a substantial difference was toe

PROVISION OF MEALS FOR SCHOOL CHILDREN for which £4OO was provided. During the past winter trade bad been good in the town an,., as tar as could be seen at present, the actual expenditure on meals would not have exceeded £5O. Consequently there would be a saving of £350 on that item for the financial year just closing. £4OO was again set aside as the probable amount required for the purpose during the coming twelve months. _ .

Passing to the School Management Committee's requirements, Mr. Jolley first dealt with higher eaucation. There was a reduction of £lOO in the salaries of teachers at the Secondary School and Technical Institute—due entirely to staff changes. He regretted tbat there was els') a reduction in the salaries in respect of the evening schools because it meant tht the attendance at the branch technical schools was not quite as large as some of them would like to see it. The average attendance was better than before, but the actual numbers on the registers were slightly clown. Under the head of scholarships there was a decrease of £5O which was also to be regretted,as it was mainly due to the fact that the £lO scholarships for the elder scholars had not been made use of any- thing like so freely as was hoped. On the receipt side there had been a steady decrease of late years in the grants from customs and excise. This year they anticipated a considerably larger revenue from that source, in consequence of the action of the Chancellor of the Exchequer, to whom appeals had been made and , who last year allowed a supplementary grant to make up for the diminution of what MS popularly called. " WHISKY MONEY.

The Committee hoped to profit by the change to the extent of £450 or £4OO, because in addition to the am.,unt received for tho year now closing, the Govern- ment bad paid something on account of the previous year. Tbat did not affect the present estimates directly but it meant that in the coming year, if a similar paysient were made, they would get about £240 more than they would otherwise have received. The total deficiency on higher education was estimated at £250 less than last year. With regard to elementary education, there was an INCREASE IS TEACHERS' SALARIES of £1,045. £6OO of that was due to the cost of the boys' department at the Evelyn-street Council school, which would be an entirely new charge. The full cost of the girls' and infants' department would not be an addi- tional item of expenditure, as the staffs were being transferred from two schools which would be closed when the Council school was opened. The remaining £4OO was due to the annual scale increases, and provision was also made for an additional cookery teacher whose servicee would only be required for part of the coming year. The annual grant in respect of elementary education showed a slight decrease. For several years the committee had been running it very fine in the matter of accommodation, and a number of schools had been overcrowded. The wonder to him was that the Board of Education had not Hued them on that account—as a matter of fact they had once or twice made slight deductious in respect of one or two schools but on the whole they had dealt generously with the committee. No grant would be paid in respect of the Evelyn-street school during the coming year. The deficiency on elementary education showed an increase of £1,350. Mr JOLLEY passed in rapid review the items of , expenditure of £2OO for professional services in super- vision of school buildings and .S4OO for medical inspection—explaining that these were matters of adjustment between Corporation departments, whereby the Education Committee bore its fair share of toe salaries of officials who devoted part of their time to its work. The

NEW DENTAL CLINIC would cost £l9O to establish. There was an increase in the deficiency of the Medical Inspection Committee's estimate of £173. He did not imagine anyone would grudge that portion of the outlay. In connection with the Finance Committee's estimates Mr Jolley touched upon an increase of £351 in rates and taxes, mainly due to a re-assessment of school premises. Provision had been made for an increase of £63 in the cost of insurance but it was not yet known what the amount would be. They would, however, have some additional responsibility put upon them in con- nection with

THE INSURANCE ACT, and the amount estimated was as near as could be judged The Rev. JAMES DAVIES : It is the insurance of the children?

Mr JoLLEV : No; the officials. He next explained that there would be a substantial increase—about £l,ooo—in the amount required for the repayment of loans for the provision of new elementary schools That was due to the fact that the Evelyn-street school building instalments had to be provided for, also part payments in respect of the Bolton school exten- sion of the boys' department, and in respect of the sites for four new schools in the Howley district, Oliver-street, Oakwood-avenue and Lovely-lane. There was an estimated increase in the Finance Committee's deficiency of £l,lOO. Coming to the summary of the estimates, Mr. Jolley said some of the members might regard it as a scorpion with the sting in its tail, but they had to face it as frankly and cheerfully as they could. It showed an increase in the deficit of £2,815 for which the rate- payers were responsible. That was the true increase in the expenditure as compared with the closing year. There was, however, AN INCREASE IN THE CALL UPON THE BOROUGH RATE of £3,500. Last year £1,250 17s. of the estimated expenditure was provided for out of the working balance, which, in the opinion of the Finance Com- mittee was then depleted as much as was safe. If anything they were cutting it a little bit too fine. Had It not been for the fact that £350 less was spent on the feeding of school children, and that £4OO was unexpectedly received to supplement the ,shortage of whisky money the working balance would have been brought to a dangerously low point. In the circumstances the Finance Committee thought it might be safe to take £571 17s from the working balance, and call for £21,000 which amount was equiva- lent to Is 9 in the 2on the estimated yield or the borough rate. The £20,500 called for in 1911-12 was equal to a rate of Is 71r1 in the so that there was an estimated increase of 21d in the 2 on the borough rate. If instead of depleting the working balance, the full cost of education each year had been charged against the rate the demand for the closing financial year would have teen Is. B,lcl in the .C. and for the coming year the rate would have been Is 10d instead of Is 9Ad. There- fore the true increase was really equal to licf in the 2, but owing to the fact that the working balance had been used the additional burden on the rate was In ten years, continued Mr. Jolley, the burden on the rates bad more than doubled. They began with a rate of approximately 103 in the £. and now it was really equal to is 9J; in the £. For the first year of their work the expenditure was £33,433, and for the tenth year it was £55,727. The borough rate contributed in the first year £9,290 and in the tenth year it would contribute £24,000. The Government grants in the first year were £18,696, whilst in the tooth year £29,700 was estimated for. There was an approximate increase in the expen- diture of 07 per, cent. ; an increase in the Government grants of 59 per cent. ; and AN INGREABE OP 158 PER CENT. IN THE RATE CONTRIBUTIoNS.

Councillor, HENSHALL : Shame.") Without raising any political issue Mr. Jor,rav referred to thl3 tendency of Parliament issue, a number of years to place additional burdens on local authorities without providing grants from Imperial sources. If there was any service which could be called national in the best and highest sense of the word that service was surely education work. He urged that local authorities were entitled to look for much snore assist- ance from Government grants in meeting increasingly heavy expenditure. After all, the shoulders of the

taxpayer were broader and stronger than those of the ratepayer. He was bound thankfully to admit that the present Chancellor had put a few golden drops into their almost empty bucket, and he hoped it was the beginning of better things. Looking at the question all round nobody could deny that there wes still

A GREAT DEAL OF LEEWAY to be made up in Warrington. Although they had done a great deal they had not done as much as some thought they ought to have done. He should never forget the words used by Mr. Fairclough in the opening year of their work—words of wisdom which had they been acted upon would have benefitted the whole community. Mr. Fairclongh then enunciated the policy that for some years to come, Warrington should provide

ONE NEW SCHOOL PER YEAR in order to meet its responsibilities in anything like the proper manner. The ninth sear was rapidly draw- ing to a close, but unfortunately they were a very long way off seeing the opening of the ninth new school. He did not suggest that if that policy had been carried out Warrington by now would have been an educational paradise. He did not suppose it would be so in the life time of anyone present. Whatever they might think about obtaining increased help from the Imperial exchequer, their immediate duty was to meet their obligaticns locally to the best of their ability and they could not do better than to vote as cheerfully as they could for the estimates, asking the Town Council to provide £24,000 out of the borough hate and looking upon that as an instalment of what they owed to the rising generation and also as an investment which in the highest sense was both productive and remunerative. (Hear, bear). Mr. KINGDON Seconded the adoption of the estimates Alderman BENNETT said it came rather as a shock to find the estimates for the current year so much higher than those for the previous twelve months, but as they had listened to Mr. Jolley's seductive explanation they realized that whatever else might be said the increased expenditure was more or less inevitable. The trouble was that the increase would probably continue for some years to come. He lamented that, but after all it would be the greatevt mistako in the world to slacken educa- tional enterprise simply because that enterprise might cause some little progressive rise in the rates. Of course they did not wish to see the rates high, but to sacrifice the education of the rising generation for the sake of a little economy in the rates would be infinitely worse. The point was to be reasonably economical, but , not to economise in the brain and life of the coming generation. The suggestion that larger grants should be paid from the Imperial Exchequer sounded very tempting, but he could not help feeling that it was somewhat illusory. The same people broadly and substantially had to pay, but what meal authorities had a right to urge was that the whole basis of local taxation should be drastically re-organized. Obviously the fairest method was a universal and steadily graduated income tax. The CHAIRMAN : That is a little bit outside the scope of the discussion. BEWSEY WARD Li

BERALS,

SUCCESSFUL SOCIAL GATHERING.

THE FIGHT FOR HOME RULE.

There was another highly successful gathering of I liberals at the Winmarleigh Cafe on Saturday evening I when the members of the Bewsey Ward Committee I were entertained at' dinner at the invitation of Coun- cillors O. Tools and J. Dickenson. There was an attendance of about 100,Councillor 0. Toole presiding. Amongst others present were Councillors J. Dickensi and. J Skelland, Messrs. J. Healey, P. 11. Brown, H. New and E. A. Sanders. After dinner an interesting programme of music and speeches was gone through, the proceedings being characterised by great enthusiasm. The CHAIRMAN said that was the first time he had had the honour of presiding officially at a Liberal function. During the whole of his life he bad been associated with the Liberal movement in Warrington. He had not actually been a member of the party but he bad never yet been opposed to it—partioularly at Parliamentary electicna. (Applause). Any reason that he had had in the past for not being an official Liberal had now gone, because the time had passed when any progressive could afford to stand aside. Nationalists and Liberals must close their ranks and march forward to attain the object for which Liberals had been fight- ing for the past twenty-six years. That was why he felt his course to be clear ard he hoped the committee would never regret the confidence they had reposed in him. He could assure them that as long as he held the position he would do his utmost not only on behalf of Liberali.m in Warrington, but for everything that affected the interests of the workers of Bewsey Ward. (Applause.) Bewsey was one of the most influential wards in the borough, although numerically it was one of the smallest. It contained the largest ratepayers in the town—the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, the proprietors of one of the greatest industries, many of whose employees were assocrated with the Liberal movement. The interests of the ward were industrial rather than residential, and it did not lend itself to development in the direction of playgrounds and bowling greens to the same extent as other wards. Councillor Dickenson was the deputy-chairman of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee and whenever a scheme for open spaces was brought forward he always had an•eye on I the requirements of Bewsey. He had not been able to arrange for a recreation ground in the ward itself, hut there would he two adjacent—in Winwick-road and Lovely-lane, where it was hoped that bowling greens would be provided. Turning to the political situation, Councillor Toole said that never in the history of Liberalism bad they had more cause for pride and rejoicing than to-day. After the long fight they had waged for progress in face of the opposi- tion of class interests, they were ON TIIE THRESHOLD OP VICTORY. Not in the lifetime of the oldest man present had the rank and file of the party felt such confidence in the Leaders as was reposed iii the statesmen who occupied the front Bench to-day. (Applause.) They admired the stamina, coolness and foresight of Mr. Asquith—(applause)—the pluck and perseverance of Mr. Lloyd George—(applause)—who, notwithstanding the most tyrannical opposition any statesman had encountered, was fighting for the masses —(applause)—and the courage of Mr. Winston Church- bill—(applause)—And many others he could name. With regard to the Insurance Act,cold water had been thrown upon it, as upon all the measures which the Liberal party had introduced for the benefit of the workers. The masses were to get some financial advantage and somebody had to pay for it. Some interested parties were up in arms against it—as they were against the Licensing Bill—but whether they relished the Act or not they would have to have it. (Applause.) He was pleased that, after the arduous fight which the Liberals had consistently waged for Home Rule since the days of Gladstone—(applause)—and after the sacrifices they had been called upon to make, they were at last to see the fruition of their labours. The horn-blowing and drum-heating that was taking place in Ulster was something like the Hyde Park demonstration against the Licensing Bill and the recent bugle-blowing in Warringtou in connection with the dumping exhibition—it was all wind. (Laughter.) No Act that had ever been passed that would do so much for the best interests of the Empire as the granting of aelf-government in domestic affairs to Ireland. The old bogeys of separation and persecution of the minority were again being trotted out. Like the dumping exhi- bition, they were designed to draw the minds of the people away from the real issue. Every sensible person knew that even in the interests of Ireland herself, it would not he wise or judicious to think of such a thing as separation. (Applause.) If he had 100 votes be would not cast a solitary one in favour of any measure that would drag Ireland any further away from England than she was to-day. (Applause.) It was their idea that not only Ireland, but Scotland, Wales, England, Australia, Canada, South Africa and all the other dependencies of the Empire should have their local Parliaments, and that in Loudon there should be an Imperial Parliament that would govern the whole of them under the Sovereignity of the Ring. (Applause.) That was all they asked for and he believed those who had striven so loyally to obtain Home Rule for Ireland would shortly see the benefits to the country of that great reform. (Applause.) The progressives in War- rington had a great deal to fight, when they considered the forces that were arrayed against the working- classes—because, after all, the Liberal party was composed of the workingclasses. They had not all the wealth and influence of the other side ; they were not able to bring the same pressure to bear. They could only PLEAD ON THE GROUND OF COMMON SENSE.

They hoped the efforts that were being made by the Liberal party and the honest convictions expounded by Mr. A. 11. Crostield—(applause)—would win hack Warrington at the next opportunity. (Applause.) When one considered the policy and tactics of the Tories, it passed one's comprehension that they should have succeeded in an industrial town like Warrington. The Liberal party was fighting for the benefit of the masses. Warrington was not an aristocratic place like Chester or Southport, its population consisting almost entirely of the working classes. Therefore the interests of the workers were most important in Warrington. It was an industrial borough and ought to be represented by an industrial man—and that man was Mr. Crosfield. (Applause.) Bewsey Ward had always been in the fore- front of Liberal activities. It had never sent to the Town Council, on a straight ticket, a councillor who was opposed to Liberalism. He sincerely hoped that when next they were called upon to do battle for the Liberal cause, that ward would not be lacking and that when Mr. Crostleld was returned—as he would be at the next election—(applause)—they would be able to claim a share in that great victory. (Applause.) Councillor DICKENSON said that during the seven rears he had represented Bewsey Ward be had tried, to the best of his ability, to do his duty to the working classes. When he entered the Council he never for one

moment expected to represent the" upper ten," but he had always stood by the workingmen and should continue to do so. They would know, from his actions in the Council, that what be had said was perfectly right and he bad endeavoured to meet opposition like a man. (Applause.) Councillor SKELLAND Said he came out as an independ- ent and he was still an independent in municipal affairs hut at the same time he was a Liberal to the backbone. He believed that the more independent Liberals—men who would do their duty fearlessly—they had in the Council, the better it wonld be for the workers. No one was more disappointed than be (the speaker) when Mr. Crosfield was defeated and he earnestly hoped Warrington would reverse its verdict at the next election. The present Government was, In his +opinion, the best that had ever been in power. (Applause.) When they had such brilliant men at the head of affairs, it was the duty of all progressives to try and keep them in office. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN, in calling upon Mr. Harry New, said bat during the short time he had been in Warrington be bad brought the fighting line into a state of effici- ey and his efforts were already bearing fruit. He vras certain that if Mr. NPVJ remained here, Warrington 'would be represented in Parliament by a Liberal. Applause.) Mr. NEw, who bad a hearty reception, briefly touched 'won tbe events of the past twelve months, beginning With the Parliamentary election. Mr. Crosfleld, he said, was a real friend of labour and an out-and-out .democrat. (Applvuse). Many of those present had, as rade unionists, bad the opportnnity of conferring with him upon the question of a tthiversal eight-hours' day -icontlnuons processes. Mr. Crosfield had travelled widely and bad come into contact with many eminent sten with reference to that question, and he had a real , and sincere desire to see the working conditions •:improved (Applause.) He was still pursuing his work in , direction and on two occasions had met the , epresen`atives of the trade unions in Warrington.

THE VIEW OF THE MAN IN THE STREET

was that in all the departments of civics and national expenciture there were few things so open to doubt as to whether we really got what, we were paying for. When one read what Sir John Gorst had recently said about the tailors of the Education Act.— _.

The CIIIRMAN : Anything that has a distinct and direct reference to the estimates is quite in order, but anything which refers in general terms to the question of whether education is valuable or not, or whether it is being properly conducted, is outside the scope of the discussion.

Councillor PEMBERTON : It is a serious matter for the public. Councillor HENSHALL : I submit that the expenditure is determined absolutely by policy. The CHAIRM AN : Our policy is limited to a question of. administration. NVe have nothing to do with the principles involved in the policy. Councillor ilktisnALL thought the man in the street would be asking whether they were justified in calling for £24,000 when he read a speech by Sir John Gorst, one of the greatest authorities on the subject, saying that a great deal of money was wasted ; toot we were missing the spirit of the Education Act, and that what the present policy seemed to be aiming at was to make

- - A NATION OF CLERKS, and very poor ones too, insteading of upholding the dignity of labour. The CHAIRMAN I say emphatically that those questions are outside the scope of the minutes under consideration. Councillor HENSHALL : I think it is not unfair that representatives of the people who have to find the money should have an opportunity of expressing them- selves on the general policy. If you rule me out of order, I can only say that you are missing a very good speech. (Laughter.) Alderman JAM 55 EVANS : Then it is no use offering fair and legitimate ci iticism of these estimates. The CHAIRMAN : I think that is not a falr conclusion to come to.

—.-- ----- The CHAIRMAN: If you will give a reason for it, I shall be glad to hear It. Councillor PEMBERTON: I think the rates are excessive and that the ratepayers of this town already have plenty to bear. Speaking to his amendment, Councillor PEMBERTON said he did not share Mr. Jolley's regret that the scholarships were not being taken up, because they had been found to be useless. A plumber who had under- gone a course at the technical school told him that when he showed his certificates they were actually detrimental to him in getting work. " Commercially, we are wasting money on education," added the Coun- cillor. "If we are going on the same in another 10 years the rate will be 3s. 9d. When are we going to stop ?'' Councillor HENSHALL When we are ruined.

Councillor PEMBERToN also maintained that the rate- payers, the majority of whom were workingmen, ought not to be called upon to find such a large proportion of the outlay on education. There was no seconder to the amendment, and the estimates were then approved. TEACEIERS' SALARIES.

The School Management Committee re-affirmed their resolution that the scale of the salaries for teachers in the public elementary schools in the Borough, which was adopted by the committee at the meeting on the 21th May, 190 G, be amended as follows, viz. :—The portion of condition 4 on page sof the scale which deals with the annual increments for teachers serving in classes C and I, to be so altered as to provide for the rate of increase to be £5 per hiIDUM, and, after 9 increases, £lO per annum, instead of £5 per annum throughout as at present. Canon STEVENSON in moving the adoption of the minute said it was submitted as an endeavour to help three teachers who belonged to a grade from which no more appointments would be made. They were:not trained teachers hut had passed the certificate examination— although in a low class—and were doing honest good work. Because of their grade however, they were only entitled under the scale to increments of £5 per annum and in one case it would take 19 years to reach the maximum. The committee therefore proposed a very small change in the scale so that after nine years service the increments would be increased to £lO per annum instead of £5. _ _

Coupe'lloi BELLIAN seconded the adoption of the minute which was agreed to.

I lost my sight and speech, and was too weak to walk. I could not sleep or eat, my only food for weeks being milk, and my life was a burden. ===== 0002601_19120224_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
[BY REDCAP].

SEASONABLE NOTES.

You are doubtless anxious to have good broods of chicks and to have them early. Much disappoint- ment follows when the broody hen does not bring off a good batch. Therefore to save this disappointment you should purchase a few days'old chicks and place them under the broody hen directly she has finished hatching. If this is done at the right moment she cannot detect the little strangers and thus you have the satisfaction of knowing that you will be well fixed for early chickens. This method can be applied with profit with those hens you are about to set. Place your orders early and have them dispatched so as to reach you when your own hens are about to hatch. If you set, say about four hens at once, even if you have poor hatches you can make them up into good broods by this method. This practice ' obviates the necessity of purchasing a foster-mother •, and to backyarders and small farmers it is a useful and seasonable "tip." Others in a larger way of business are bound—for commercial reasons—to adopt artificial methods of rearing.

TIM lIREEDING PENS. Keep a strict eye on the breeding pens. If any of the hens have gone broody, replace them with reserve stock birds. Any ailing ones should also be removed and treated. If for reasons of the severe weather you have not mated large pens, you may now add with safety a few extra females. Give your stock birds an occasional feed of lean meat scraps. Avoid all patent spices, as this tends to injure the fertility of the eggs. A box containing a supply of crushed oyster shell and old spent mortar should be placed in the pen. Lime in some form is absolmely essential, especially to hens in a confined run.

SPECKLED SUSSEX. Red, brown, speckled and light. It matters little what colour you choose. The very name itself implies quality. For surely everyone will know that Sussex is the home of utility fowls. It is of the fowls however we must speak, and not of the county. Suas-x fowls can be termed the ideal farmer's fowl. They are good layers and excellent table birds. In all the colours I have mentioned you get them, but all have the much desired white shanks, this together with the fine quality of flesh they carry makes them at once most useful for culinary purposes ; Sussex also are excellent winter layers and to my knowledge have already won in laying competitions. For the cottager, farmer and small holder they are a most useful general purpose fowl. lam glad to say that the Sussex fowls are making rapid strides in the Northern oounties. What is good for the London market is also welcome in our Northern centres. Next week we shall display a pair of Rhode Island Reds.

ITICKEN MANAGEMENT. (Continued). Warmth and activity are the two essentials necessary to successful chicken management. gaily people forget that fowls are domesticated creatures, and that under ordinary natural conditions no chickens would be seen ere March had gone at least. Therefore you will observe the need for special attentton to their wants in this direction. The best way to keep chickens active is by burying a few handfuls of dry chick food in thp scratching material. So long as there is a morsel to be found, on they will scratch. And you can afford them no better form of exercise than this. Keep them on the move. If your rearing accommoda- tion is very open and bleak, then you should erect temporary shelter. A very useful and inexpensive method is as follows :--Make the framework of a small shed with ordinary wooden hurdles, and intertwine them with wheat straw. These should be so placed that neither north or east wind can get inside. When the weather is bad such a contrivance makes a very welcome retreat, and will often be fre- quented by the chicks, especially if you adopt the advice given by covering the floor with chaff and burying in the chick food therein, and further, these places serve another purpose. As the season advances and when the sun gets more powerful the chickens are able to seek shelter from the hot rays of the sun. I only mention this fact to prevent their too hasty removal. Let them remain until the rearing season is completely ended, then you can dismantle the hurdles and store them for another season.

SEPARATING TIIE SEXES.

As soon as you are able to discern the sexes you should separate them. There is no special time when this should be done—you will have to be guided by observations,—but so soon as you are able to make deductions them separate at once. The young stock always thrive better as a result. Chickens from the lighter breeds mature very much quicker than the heavy breeds and are thus easier to detect. Ann with these it is especially important that early separation should be seen too. Black Leghorns for instance are a very precocious breed and if not taken in hand early, very soon deteriorate in stamina as a result of being allowed to mix. Take this instance, and apply it in other breeds according to developements, then you cannot get far wrong. GENERAL REMARKS. When feeding chicks, c4re should be taken to avoid sour food. See that all utensils are kept scrupulously clean. In fact, these should have a wash in Condy's fluid about every other day. Same substitute for insect life should also be supplied. Minced beef or finely ground green bone are good, but for preference minced meat is the best for young chicks. If you notice any signs of diarrhma, leave off the animal food at once and feed chiefly on oatmeal and milk. CHICKEN lINEASKS. At all times keep a strict look out for any ailing ones. Gapes are known to be a very general ailment amongst chickens. And some ground is very liable to give this dreadful disease. Prevention is better than cure and when chickens are given finely chopped onions in the soft food they will rarely be attacked with gapes. Should however any of the flock get an attack you should treat them at once. There are quite a number of suggested remedies. Roup powder is very effectual when applied in the early stages of the disease. This Iwder should be given in the drinking water, and no other water should be accessible. At first the chicks may refuse to drink but they will soon come to it in time.

===== 0002601_19120224_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
COLDS.

This is a very common complaint, especially at this period of the year. If not arrested it soon spreads through the whole flock. The symptons are easy to detect. And as soon as you notice any sneezing and watery discharge from the nostrils you should treat them for cold. A very simple and cheap remedy is Condy's Fluid take a small vessel filled with warm water and add a teaspoonful of the fluid. Then

OOT TILL A I t 47.31 (/ Ana CP" t 44 1 i- ir tiLeTIC NOTES BY " OVALIST."

Events have moved rapidly since last Saturday. Warrington have played Normanton St. John's twice, and after drawing with difficulty at Normanton they woo the re-play by a huge score of 75 points to nil. Then came the draw for Round 2, and the fateful verdict that Warrington must go to Hull to meet Kingston Rovers at Craven-street. It is worth noting that for two seasons Warrington have not been drawn at home in a Cup-tie. Last season they visited Coventry and Wigan, and succumbed in the second round. The prospect is not much brighter this time, but the Wirepullers will go to Bull with more hope and confidence than they would to Wigan.

On the season's form the Rovers are the superior side, and have proved a very hard nut to crack at Craven-street. If I am not mistaken the Australasians are the only team which has won there this season. W arrington have once previously met the Rovers in a cup-tie—the final at Headingley in 190.5—when they won by 6 points to nil. Perhaps it is the Rovers' turn, but I would not like to predict with any confidence much before 4.50 p.m. next Saturday. The Wirepullers will be triers to a man, and their chances are never to be despised in a cup. tie.

===== 0002601_19120224_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DRAW FOR THE SECOND ROUND.

The draw for the second round of the Northern Union Challenge Cup was made at Manchester on Tuesday afternoon, and resulted as follows : Hull Kingston Rovers v Warringten.—Referee : W McCutcheon (Oldham). Touch judges: E J Kilvingston (Leeds), and A Brown (Wakefield). Oldham v Wigan Hull v Rochdale Hornets Leigh v Huddersfield Barrow v Batley Salford v Dewsbury Halifax v. Brought on Rangers Keighley v Wakefield Trinity. Ties to be played on Saturday, March 2nd.

The very ordinary character of the first-round, in which the leading \ teams were kept apart and the junior and minor sides dismissed, has already proved a boon and a blessing. The benficial effects are show 13 in the above draw, which could scarcely have been improved upon, even if the authorities had bad a free hand in the matter. Fate could not have decreed better. Sixteen clubs, equally representative of Lancashire and Yorkshire, went into the bat, and the draw has neither favoured the Palatine nor the Broadacres, for each shire has been alloted four games. Further, it will be'noted that in all the ties save two the contesting parties will be rival county teams. Warrington end Rochdale Hornets of the sixteen are the only teams drawn away in each round. On the other hand, Hull, Leigh, Barrow and Halifax have twice played at home. The unfortunate feature is that both Hull teams are at home.

The spectators at Wilderspool on Monday were loud in their enquiries as to what Warrington had been doing at Normanton. It did seem a fact wanting some explanation that the team which could only draw on Saturday should beat the same opponents on Monday by 75 points to nil. I can assure my readers that Normanton St. Johns at home and at Wilderspool were two vastly different teams in a playing sense. On Monday they would probably had been beaten by the "A " team, but on Saturday they played a game which would have troubled any opposi- tion and came very near to the undoing of Warring- ton. These collier lads were veritable lions on their own turf and before an excited crowd of pit-men. It seems difficult to believe, but that light pack of forwards which so soon collapsed on Monday, played the full SO minutes on Saturday at a pace which even Warrington men oould not withstand. They beat the Warrington man for possession to such no extent that the crowd was laying long odds every scrum that Normanton got the ball. When they did, the halves drove it along the ground and the whole team went "hell for leather" after it. Luckily the Warrington backs were all sound and fearless tacklers, for they never had a warmer time on the defence. The Normanton lads spent nearly 30 minutes within five yards of the Warrington goal-line, but they never once crossed. Warrington came well out of the struggle with two tries against three goals—an equality of points.

I felt quite sure that the visitors on Monday would collapse after the first score, but I never anticipated them lying down to the extent they did. Twenty-five points in the first half, and fifty in the second was a record that Warrington has only once beaten—on April 12, 1909, when they beat St. Helens by 0 goals, 20 tries, 78 points to nil, just a try more than on Monday. But for Referee Renton's strict interpreta- tion of forward passing, the score on Monday would have been near three figures. The home forwards played a great game, Fearnley, Skelborn and Tranter showing fine pace and combination, while Dickenson and Renwick excelled themselves at centre.

To-day Warrington resume League football, and as they visit Rochdale they can discuss the prospects of the visit to Hull which they both have to make next Saturday. Warrington have frequently done well at the Athletic Grounds, and it would be an excellent omen for the following s eek if they could win to-day. But "I hae ma doots." ••••••MMO

The selected team is: Mclntyre, full back; Woodburn, Renwick, Dickenson and Clare, three- quarters; Jordan and Brooks, half backs ; Thomas, Shugars, Cheaters, Fearnley, Tranter and Skelhorn, forwards.

Daintith sustained slight concussion on Monday, and is having a rest. I am rather sorry that for to-day's "A ' team match, Tranter has not been given a trial at centre. He is developiog much cleverness as well as pace in the loose, and there seems to me the makings of a threequarter in the tall Warrington lad. The "A ' team to meet Rochdale "A " will contain Jolley, the young full-back who played such a remark- able game in the junior final against ftylands' Recs., and is a nephew of Jim Jolley of Runcorn fame. J. Butterworth's younger brother will also appear at centre, so that there seems to be a tendency for the Runcorn juniors to migrate to Warrington.

The "A" team will be: Jolley, full-back' Waywell, Butterworth, Brown, and Povey, three- quarters; Chester and Vernon, half-backs; Boardman, Critchley, Arnold, Bradshaw, Cooper and Clark, forwards.

LANCASHIRE JUNIOR CUP FINAL.

RYLANDS RECS V RUNCORN WHITE STAR. There was a capital crowd on the St. Helens ground last Saturday to witness the final between the above clubs. About 200 spectators accompanied the Rylands team from Warrington, but they had to return disappointed. Runcorn started and neat combination threatened the Recs' line. Bramball fielded well and Rylands' took up the running. Leah followed up a big kick and dribbled over, registering a fine try for Rylands'. He failed to add the goal points. Keen football was seen at centre, but the general run of play was in favour of the Rylands' team, whose backs were smart and repeatedly gained possesion. A fine move- ment on the part of the Runcorn threequarters gave them a footing in the Rylands' territory. Here Bramhall intercepted a pass, but retained possession of the leather too long. He was tackled and the ball came to Thornton, who made the scores level. The goal kick failed. Fast open play followed and each team pressed in turn, but the defence proved sound. Interval : Runcorn White Star, three points ; Rylands' Recreation, three points. Rylands' re-opened with a fine movement, but .Jolley handled finely and found touch well up the field. The Runcornians asserted themselves and for a long period were better behind than Rylands'. The defence of the Warrington team was thoroughly sound. Fearnett inter- cepted a pass and ran over grandly at the corner. Runcorn had the better of play to the finish and a goal was added. Result : Runcorn White Star, one goal two tries (eight points) ; Rylands' Recreation one try (three points). After the game the cup was presented to the Runcorn team. On their arrival home they were met by a number of supporters and the trophy carried round the town in triumph. RYLANDS' RECS. V. SALFORD TRINITY.—At Salford to-day. Team :—Full back,A Bramhall ; three-quarters, J Griffiths, W Leah (capt.), J Hennessey, F Horrocks, half backs ; J Gallop, G Floyd ; forwards, A Stretford, Branwood, A Booth, H Morris, F Mason, J Broadhurst.

Warrington V. Normanton St. John's.

TWO REMARKABLE GAMES. Warrington took a full team, and about 100 spectators to Norruanton on Saturday, to meet the St. John's Club in the first round. The Saints were reckoned no mean foes on their own ground, and having knocked out Outwood Church and Featherstone in the preliminary rounds, they were fully prepared to give Warrington a warm welcome at Mopsey Garth. Beyond a slight drizzle, which soon ceased, the weather was pleasant, and the ground in good order. Both sides fielded their selected thirteens, and an excited crowd of about 2,000 (mostly colliers) lined the rails at 3.39. Mr Tonge (Swinton) was the referee, and the teams were : Warrington : Mclntyre. full back; Bradshaw, Renwick. Dickenson and Woodburn, threequarters ; Daintith and Brooks, half backs ; Thomas, Shugars, Cheaters, Treater, Fearnley and Skelhorn, forwards. Normanton St. John's ; White, full back ; Ta;lor, Goodfellow, Stones and Nicholson. threermarters ; Buckley and Ward, half backs ; O'Neill. Stringer, Willis, Walker, Kilcomrnons and Bedworth, forwards. Warrington won the toss and played with the breeze behind them, though against a considerable slope. Goodfellow started and the Saints at once got. offside in their eagerness. The first scrum gave Warrington possession and the halves sent the ball along to Woodburn, who was grassed with a certainty that showed the intentions of the horuesters to allow no latitude. The Normanton forwards rushed the next pack, and Buckley snapping the ball up started a dribble which carried playto the Warrington line, Mclntyre having to throw the ball dead to save. Great cheering and excitement followed as the locals pinned Warring- ton near the line. Buckley and Ward were very nippy, and dashing footwork kept Warrington very busy on the defence. Bradshaw relieved with a plucky break-away to the quarter line, and a penalty kick by Renwick cleared the invaders out. The respite was only momentary, for the homesters played the kick and rush game to perfection, and the Warring- ton backs were unable to get going at all. Goodfellow twice took penalty shots at goal in the Warrington quarter, but missed each time from easy positions. A great effort by the Warrington farwards drove Normanton back again and for once in sway the halves got the ball out and a round of passing saw Bradshaw bundled in touch at the corner. Again Warrington handled and Woodburn was given a chance on the other side, but though be raced away he could not beat the full-back with yards of room. These were the only two chances Warrington had this half, for the Saints got down to the line again as the result of a smart break- away and follow-up by Buckley. Fierce work followed on the Warrington line, the home partisans cheering every inch gained. Buckley, Ward and Taylor all tried to force their way over, but the tackling of Maclntyre Brooks and Daintith was magnificent. The defence held out in wonderful fashion until Stones got the ball ten yards from the line and dropped a smart goal. The crowd cheered this opening success deliriously but Warrington bad escaped lightly in their own estimation The drop-out gave them a footing at the other end and Renwick tried a pot at goal which failed, and the interval then arrived with Normanton leading by 2 points to nil. .

The game so far had been vastly in the borne team's favour, but Warrington resumed in a way which inspired hope. The forwards got their heads downhill and swept the Saints before them to the line. Daintith and Brooks opened out smartly hut Renwick mulled the pass with a nice opening. Normanton fought their way to the centre again, but Warrington got possession and Brooks reads a characteristic burst through the backs. When near the till back he threw to Woodburn who shook off Nicholson and raced over in good position, but Renwick failed at goal Once ahead, it looked as if Warrington must win easily, and some fast passing enabled Bradshaw to get within a yard of the line. A penalty gave the Saints relief and Goodfellow finding touch near the line, excitement again ran high. The scrum was rushed in true Yorkshire style and Thomas had to pass back to Mclntyre to save. The latter tried to punt clear from behind the goal, but the ball went high and Stones marked right opposite, Goodfellow kicking a goal, and putting Normanton in front once more. Again the battle waged fast and furiously. Warrington were deter- mined and Brooks again got away, but Dickenson's pass to Woodburn was knocked on and the winger bad to come back after running over. Daintith made a desperate dash from the next pack but was grassed inches short. The pressure on the home line was kept up grandly, and the Warrington halves again got away smartly. Renwick took a fast pass beautifully and raced over, a dozen yards from touch. This time Thomas took the kick but failed. For a timeffarriugton maintained theladvantager Tranter and Skelhorne shining with a fine passing run. The homesters were persistent, however, and Bradshaw had to mark under the posts to save his side. Good- fellow again found touch near the Warrington line, and the last flve minutes were exciting. Warrington broke up the pack, but were penalised, and a minute from the finish Goodfellow robbed Warrington of the lead and made the result a draw with a fine goal. The cheering of the home crowd was loud and long. Final : rington. 2 tries (6 points) ; Normanton St, John's, 3 goals (6 points).

THE RE-PLAY. Warrington's Mighty Score. The replay was quickly arranged and Norman' on St. John's appeared at Wilderepool on Monday afternoon. Both sides made a couple of changes. Warrington were without Woodburn and Bradshaw. the substitutes being Waywell and Glare, while the Saints displaced Stones for Bruce, and Stringer for Bailey. Owing to the short notice of the match, the crowd was small, about 1,500 paying for admission. Mr Renton was referee. Normanton started in plucky style, but the forwards seemed to have lost their fire and the result was never in doubt. Warrington were early in possession and the backs handling beautifully Dickenson sent Clare over twice in the first ten minutes. Buckley made an effort which took play to the Warrington quarter,but this was practically the visitors' last chance. INaywell raced over near the posts, after the halves and Renwick had handled finely, and Mclntyre kicked a goal. Before the interval Fearnley rushed over twice after fine following up, and further tries were scored by Dickenson and Renwick, the latter kicking one goal. Half-time, Warrington 2 goals, 7 tries (25 points). In the second half, though Daintith was carried off injured, Warrington overwhelmed the visitors. The latter seemed to give up the ghost completely, and fifty points were put on by Warrington in forty minutes. The snorers were Skelhorn (3), Renwick, Mestere (2), Mclntyre, Shugars, Dickenson, Brooks, Fearnley and Clare, goals being added by Mclntyre, Renwick (2), Thomas, Cheaters, Shelhorn and Clare. Final : War- rington 9 goals, 19 tries (75 points), Normanton St. John's nil. ST. HELENS GO DOWN AT BARROW.

At Cavendish Park, B;r;c;1:v, before nearly 6,000 spectators, the gate amounting to £llB. The game was fast throughout, and the Barrow forwards were smart in their following up and tackling. The backs, too, main- tained their recent improvement in combined work, and Narey, the ex-Keighley half back, was very successful. In the first half both sides had turns of aggression, and then Natey kicked a penalty goal for the home side, the only score in this portion. Barrow took up the running on the resumption, and with Turtill beaten Bamber I scored an unimproved try near touch. Wyatt was next I overhauled by Dobson, and then Mitchell broke away

pus passed to Dobson, who scored in a good position. Narey added the goal points. The St. Helens backs combined prettily but not effectively, but success came eventually through Briers, the try being unimproved. Treloar scored again for Barrow. Result :—Barrow 2 goals 3 tries a 3 points), St Helens 1 try (3 points). Teams and officials : Barrow—Morgan, back ; Ember, Dobson, Mitchell, , and Treloar, three-quarter backs ; Narey and Barnes, half backs ; Parr, Blacklock, Hailes, Wilkinson Youldon and Dixon, forwards. s St Helens —Turtill, back, Flannagan, Greenwood. Flelshaw and Wyatt. three-quarter backs ; Treunith and Molyneux, half backs ; Saunders, Prescott, Mavity, Shallcross, Briers and Waddell, forwards. Referee, Mr A Smith, Halifax. Touch judges, Messrs Barrow, Preston, and Ball, Wigan. WIDNES v. SALFORD—LOST OPPORTUNITIES. Played at Widnes, before 4,000 spectators. At the outset the play was of a very scraggy character, and neither side could claim any advantage. Salford then got down to the Widnes line, and from a scrummage close in Lally lost the ball, and May picking up scored. Mesley failed to improve. The play improved after this,and both sides displayed good football,the interval score being—Salford 1 try (3 points), to Widnes nil. Widnes opened well in the second half, and for fully a quarter-of-an-hour kept Salford on the defence. They failed to take advantage of their opportunities,however and passing between Mesley and Loveluck ended in the latter scoring at the corner. Mesley failed at the place kick. Launce failed at a catch, and a rush by the home forwards ended in Rigby scoring. In the closing stages John dropped a goal for Salford. Result— Salford 1 goal 2 tries (8 points), Widnes 1 try (3 points Teams and officials :

Widnes—Jones, back; Reid, Taylor, Dainteth, and Leas, three-quarter backs ; Lally and O'Garra, half- backs: U Aspey, 8 Aspey, Cosgrove, Higgins, Lloyd, and Rigby, forwards. Salford—Launce, back ; Cook, Thomas, Loveluck. and Mesley,three-quarter backs ; John and Dilay,half backs ; E Thomas, Rees, Pugsley, Thom, Bavan, and Ritchie, forwards.

Referee, Mr F Renton, Hunslet. Touch judges, Messrs J Hayes, Leigh, and T Austin, Wigan.

FRIENDLY MATCH.

WARRINGTON/ `1 -P-EN-Dl.-E—T-0-ti-.l:ThiS match was played at Warrington on Saturday belore a fair attend- ance, a trial being given to Jackson and Chester (local) and Blackburn (Oldham). The match was well contested and at half time there was no score. Early in the second portion Clare initiated a movement which led up to Brown, the full-back, scoring, the goal-kick failing. The home team now monopolised the game, and Vernon made an opening for Clare to score under the posts, Critchley converting. Boardman was the next to score, the resultant goal kick failing. At the close the visitors made a determined attack on the Warrington line, but were kept at bay. Final: Warrington "A " 1 goal 3 tries, 11 points, Pendleton nil.

ORDER OF DRUIDS

THE DOCTORS AND THE INSURANCE:ACT.

A successful and pleasing function took place in the Royal Oak Hotel on Monday evening, when, under the auspices of the Queen Victoria Lodge No. 455 of Druids, a large number sat down to an excellently pre- pared hot-pot supper. After the tables had been cleared a social meeting was held under the chairmanship of the District Grand Master, Bro T Derbyshire, and he was supported by the Deputy District Grand Master, Bro AV Bibby, jun.; D.T., Bro R Perdue : P.D.G.M., Bro S Packer; P.D.G.M., Bro E Pointon; P.D.G.M., Bro Thos Eden ; Bro John Bibby; Lodge Treasurer, Bro R Long ; Lodge Secretary, Bro AV J Hill ; District Auditor, Bro A Shaw; Dr F H Gandhi and about 70 members.

The DISTRICT TREASURER (Bro. R Perdue) in giving the toast " The Order of Druids and the ‘tiarrington District," said that the present time would rank es the most vital in the histoly of friendly societies and the Order of Druids would not be behind in advancing its claims. The Order was in a flourishing condition, and he could speak personally of this district. He quoted figures to show the rapid strides the District had made financially during the last 17 years, showing that it had risen under the equalipation horn being worth less than .£5OO to over £2,000, and that after every member had received his full sick benefits. Had it not been for the system of equalisation, some of the Lodges would ere this have ceased to exist, as it was they are all in a strong financial position. (Applause). Bro. John Bibby (junior auditor of the order) in responding to the toast, said he was pleased to see such a large gathering present, as it gave him a good opportunity to speak on matters which affected each one individually. As members of Friendly Societies it was their business not to question the Insurance Bill because it was now an Act, but to study it carefully and to get from it the best possible benefits for their members. The Order of Druids had a good name to uphold and they must see to it that that naree did not leave it. They wanted the hearty co-operation of all the members,so that those in authority would be in a position to voice their views at the numerous meetings which would take place. There was one phase of the Act which was not yet settled and he wanted to draw their special attention to it. He referred to Ole doctors. Although only a young man, he could speak of a 10 years practical experience in a system such as the Act pro- posed to adopt. It appeared to him that those doctors who were revolting knew nothing or practically nothing as to how this dreaded contract system would affect them financially. Some had had a little of it but this Act provided a big bit for the doctors and it might well have been called the Doctors' Endowment Act. But, was it the financial aspect the doctors dis- like? I conclude not. They disliked the idea that Friendly Society members should be,in plain language, their employers. Friendly Society Medical Associa- tions had always treated their medical officers with the greatest respect and their experience had shown that the doctors need have no fear of the workingolasses having power on the various committees under the Act. (Applause). At an interval in the proceedings Bro. ISAAC SHAW presented, on behalf of the members a framed emblem of merit to Bro. Henry Clough. Mr. Shaw who has been a member of the Order tor fifty-five years, said that it was a gift which he, the recipient and his family, should always treasure. They were pleased to be able to make that presentation in recognition of the many valuable services Bro. Clough had rendered to that Order. (Applause). Bro. A. Sit,kw supported and added his testimony to the capabilities of the recipient. Mr. Cr.ouarr suitably responded. Ile was proud of the gift but more so because it had been handed to him by one of the oldest members of that branch of the Order. (Hear. hear.) They had got a lot of young blood in the Order and they.meant to make it a success. (Applause). The toast of the Lodge was duly proposed and honoured and a vote of thanks to the artistes was pro posed by Bro W Bibby, jun, D.P.G.M., seconded by Bro Rushton. The Chairman was also thanked on the pro- position of Mr J Bibby, seconded by Bro Edward Pointon.

_ The following artistes were responsible for a most enjoyable entertainment :—Messrs .1 Willett,S Aukland S Dayus, with Mr G Lomas as pianist.

Chester College Club. The members of the Warrington branch of the Chester College Club held a successful social gathering at the Lion Hotel on Saturday. The contributors to the musical programme were :—Messrs J Wright, W H Cragg, H H Gibbs, G F Parr and W II Rigby. Mr Theo Arden's presence at the piano was much appreciated. The following officials were elected for the year : President, Mr J Wright ; ice-president, Mr J Glover ; committee, Messrs J Armstrong, A S Lindsay, J Arnell, J E Pettener, and A Ireland; secretary and treasurer, Mr H S Smith.

LANCASHIRE EDUCATION.

Debate on Supply and Salaries of Teachers. Presiding at Monday's meeting of the Lancashire Education Committee at Preston, Sir H. F. Ilibbert referred to the resolutions passed at a conference at Leeds calling attention to the shrinkage which bad taken place in the numbers entering the teaching pro- fession, and to the consequent probability of a serious shortage in the supply of teachers in the near future. The resolutions. he said, seemed to strike at the whole root of the difficulty. The number of admissions of bursars and pupil teachers throughout England was nearly live thousand greater in 1906-7 than in 1910-11, and this would be more accentuated if Wales were not considered in the letter period. It was, therefore, quite evident that there was a very great shrinkage of young people going in for becoming elementary teachers. They would remember that last year v.hen a large number of teachers left the training colleges there was a great outcry of unemployed. Tbe fact was that a large number of teachers came out of the colleges and could not find employment, hut be submitted that there would be a similar state of affairs in the legal, medical, or any other protes3ion if all the students came out. From the statistics of Cl training colleges supplied by the N.U.T. they found that out of the total students leaving college last year 3,080 were permanently employed, 236 temporarily employed, while 249, or roughly six per cent were known to be outl of employment. He moved that the resolutions passed by the Leeds Conference be approved, ROU that copies be forwarded to the various Lancashire members of Parliament. Mr J J Cockshott said he thought that [before they asked the Board of Education to make grants for the specific purpose of the maintenance of the pupil teachers and bursars they ought to have more informa- tion than had been supplied to them. He believed the leakage in the profession had been very much lessened. Buildings had improved, classes were smaller, and salaries were very much higher, and these three things,

Mr Kinch thought it was a somewhat hasty proceed- ing to make a definite application to the Board of Education for grants for the purpose which had been mentioned. The sudden drop to 6,137 in 1910-11 from 11,01 S in 1906-7 in the number of admissions of bursars and pupil teachers was due to the fright that came over people when teachers all over the country were crying out for employment. Parents were discovering that the

TEACHING PROFESSION WAS A VERY UNCERTAIN ONE, and they were not putting their children in training for it to the same extent.

Mr S Taylor said he would vastly prefer that the Board of Eoucation should give them grants to enable them to increase the salaries of the teacher. He thought that this would not only tend to increase the number of teachers coming into the profession, but would also tend to increase the proficiency of those teachers, for they must remember that it was the teachers themselves they bad to look to for the improve- ment of the elementary education of the country. Mr. Wade Deacon said he adhered very strongly to the old opinion he had always had that if they paid adequate salaries, the teachers would provide the train- ing themselves, and they would get an adequate supply or teachers, such as they got in the medical and other professions. Sir Henry said he only proposed the resolutions as their chairman, but seeing the dangerous way the dis- cussion was going about the salaries of teachers, he thought it would be better if they had not discussed it in public at all. Members who talked about increas- ing the salaries of elementary teachers forgot that the-

PRESENT BIG EDUCATION RATE was occasioned by the increase in the salaries of teachess in 1902, consequent on the passing of the Act of tbat year. He was sorry the discussion had taken the lines it had, for it had given a very great impetus. to a union who would not fail to take advantage of the remarks that had been heard that morning. Eventually the matter was referred to the Higher Education Sub-committee.

Education.

Not the proverbial ENGAGING stupidity but a kind of SIMPLICITY, engaging simplicity is

now the note of the Tory Party. "Childlike," though far from "bland," the Tories, under the new regime introduced by the exchange of Mr. BONAR LAW for Mr.BALFOUR as leader in the House of Commons, have adopted the converse of a Christian principle with a touch- ing ingenuousness of belief. They are so thoroughly convinced that if they only smite their opponents with sufficient rude- ness on one cheek, the other will immediately and humbly be offered to their castigation. A very naive and yet typical confession of this attitude was made in the parliamentary sketch of the " Pall Mall Gazette" in describ- ing the famous debate of last week in which Mr. BONAR LAW, after a long tirade of vulgar abuse against !the Government thought he was "scoring" off Mr: Asquith by declaring, in answer to a chal- lenge, that " certainly " the Tory Party I would repeal the Insurance Act if and when it came into office. Naturally, the statement spread dismay amongst Mr. BONAR LAW'S followers, and he was driven to explain it away as far as possible by a letter to the Press. The "Pall Mall Gazette" comment was that Mr. BONAR LAW had shown how splendid he could be in attack, and Mr. ASQUITH had taken an unfair advantage of him by skilful cross- examination! The same engaging simpli- city appears in the Toiy reply, when challenged to substantiate, on the floor of the House,wild charges of corruption against the Government. They are convinced, they say in effect, that they could work up a good case if the Government would supply them with material—but of course they made the charge in order to find out whether it was true or not!

Then, after their leaders had assured the electors, all up and down the country, that the Government would, if it obtained a majority at the last General Election, use it to pass a Home Rule Bill, and after the Tory Press had exultingly proclaimed that the Liberal leaders had made this quite clear in their speeches, the Tory Party now believes that one of its best trumps to play is an accusation against the Government of having concealed from the the electors that Home Rule was before the country. More engaging simplicity! And because Mr.

BALFOUR'S idea of House of Lords' reform was to make the Second Chamber " not better, but stronger," the Tories actually expect sane men to believe that the Liberal Government has pledged itself to remove the limit on the Lords' Veto as soon as the Second Chamber was reformed. This extraordinary hallucination that the Liberal Government was pledge-bound by Mr. BALFOUR'S theories on House of Lords Reform is the sole foundation for the Tory argument that Irish Home Rule can be passed more easily before, rather than after. the creation of a reformed Second Chambe r

The decision of the WHO WILL PAY? Birmingham City Council to make the local ratepayers pay £13,155 for tram rails made in Leeds, instead of accepting the offer of the German firm at a cost of £ll,46o,recalls a dictum of Mr. A. J. Balfour. Speaking in the House of Commons, Mr. Balfour said : "I, lire everyone else in this House, desire that the Government contracts should be executed by mansfacturers and workmen in this country; but I cannot accede to the principle, as deduced from that, that we should deliberately buy in the more expensive market at the cost of the general taxpayers, when elsewhere at a cheaper rate we can get such goods as we require." The decision of its Council may be " popular" in Birmingham. But if it is to prove any- thing useful to our Tariff Reformers, then wages will go up in Birmingham to enable the local ratepayers to pay the higher price. We shall see.

Tne " Budget " state- THE COST OF ment presented by the EDUCATION. C hai r man of the Finance Committee (Mr. A. J. Jolley), at the meeting of the War- rington Education Committee on Monday evening, afforded cold comfort to the rate- payers. "Warrington has still a great deal of leeway to make up" declared Mr. Jolley and this no doubt portends that the education rate will continue on an ascending scale for some years to come. Little wonder that some members were disposed to ask: Are we getting value for this heavy expenditure? But discussion on the broader aspect of the question was ruled out of order, or much would doubtless have been said about Sir John Gorst's declaration—quoted by Councillor Henshall —that our present educational policy aims at the creation of a nation of a clerks, instead of upholding the dignity of labour. For the present the fact which most concerns the Warrington ratepayer is that the call under the head of education has increased by £3,500 which means a shade over 3d. on the education rate, as a penny in the now pro- duces £l,llO as compared with £l,OBO last year. The rate therefore stinds at about is. 91/2d. in the £ and with four new schools in immediate contemplation there can be little doubt that the amount Nill be higher next year. Particularly striking were the comparative figures which Mr. Jolley submitted, showing that the Education Committee's expenditure has risen from £33,434 in the first year of its existence to £55,727—a matter of 67 per cent. Govern- ment grants have advanced by 59 per cent., whereas the increase in the contribution from the borough rates represents 158 per cent. It is fervently hoped that the time is not far distant when the Government will come to the aid of municipalities, upon which increasingly heavy burdens have been laid in recent years, by bearing a much larger proportion of the expenditure on a national service like education through the Imperial Exchequer.

SANDNOJ

The man with a deep and cavernous coal- cellar is an object of envy in these troublous times. * * *

We still await a reply from Lady Greenall about the foreign clocks which were given as prizes at the Dump Exhibition. The clocks tick merrily on, but our Tariff Reform friends remain as silent as the grave. * *

The British rate Of unemployment in January last, as published by our Board of Trade, was only 21 per cent. This is the lowest record since the war year of 1900. Once more the " bad winter" which Mr. Bonar Law wants so badly has failed him. * *

"It is ridiculous to suppose that two shillings a quarter on corn would restore prosperity to agriculture, although the farmers might possibly support it as drown- ing men will catch at a straw."—Mr. Chamberlain to the Duke of Devon Shire.

• The death of " Paddy " Clark breaks a strong local link with the past. " Paddy" was only a humble cabby, but he was as well known to older Warringtonians as the town clock and, indeed, had a face nearly as broad as that which o'er-looks the town from Trinity tower. He was a genial soul, characteristically Irish in speech and temperament, and possessed of many uncommon recollections. _ _ r,

A letter from Mr. Alfred Radfirth, (late of Latchford) but now in France shows how much we dump into that protected country, and what a price the people have to pay for the goods, as a result of the tariff. Tea is 3s 4d per lb, and Capstan Navy Cut tobacco, 10d an ounce, while matches are so dear that the poor still resort to the flint and steel and cotton wick. All the English goods are vastly dearer than in England, but no one in France suggests that the "foreigner pays the tax." * * *

Mr T. D. Leslie, in another column, raises the question whether an increase of the number of arrests for drunkenness in Warrington, denotes an increase in drinking or merely accelerated activity on the part of the police. The question has been discussed before, but there is no answer. When we see how much better the returns are from uch a colliery centre as Wigan, how- sver, we are at liberty to conclude if we wish, ehat the police there wink at cases of drunk- nness which would result in imprisonment tt Warring ton. Figures may prove anything, eut oft-times they prove nothing. * • The skeleton of a bear was unearthed near the banks of the Ship Canal at Latchford yesterday. Unfortunately it was not a pre-historic bear or an otherwise remarkable bruin, whose remains were thus brought to light. The tale goes that about a dozen years ago a showman's performing bear was taken ill at Latchford and had to be shot. We learn that the skeleton, nicely cleaned by the hand of time, is to find a place in the Warrington Museum. It should be quite impressive with a label to the following effect : "Skeleton of bear; found at Latchford, February 23rd, 1912; indigenous to the district."
