Public health should soon make rapid advances in the direction of perfection, if there is any force in the repeated stimulus which is given to the question locally. The latest proposal is for "a national health week," in support of which an eloquent address was given before a representative audience, presided over by the Mayor, in the Council Chamber on 'Tuesday afternoon. * * Alderman Bennett's "Sunrise A nnuar has made its third appearance. It is a bright little journal, but what it seems to lack is an editor with a large blue pencil. An annual with a year's events to chronicle might be spared from misuse as an outlet for the vehe- ment political outpourings of Alderman Bennett.

* * The opening paragraph of an article entitled "The Progress of the Year," makes one shudder, with its mixed allusions to, "uninterrupted sunshine," "kingly pomp,' "bloody wars and wild unrest," " red ruin and the breaking up of laws." After this richly-coloured opening one cannot feel more than a mild surprise that the writer should incorporate the cheapest sneers of the Party he generally upholds, and charge the Govern- ment with extorting guarantees from "the new and inexperienced King" to deal with the House of Lords and obey the dictates of Mr. John Redmond.

* * But there are gems of truth in the gross mass of misrepresentation which obscures the "Sunrise." An outburst of candour is responsible for " We are strongly in favour of the Insurance Act . . . . • (though it should have been coupled with Tariff Reform "). Perhaps the qualification explains why the Alderman has not been seen or heard on the many public platforms arranged for the exposition of the Act. At any rate he hits the nail on the head now when he adds:

" We are not able to repress a feeling of con- tempt for thee who, after covering the Chancellor with fulsome praises when the Bill was introduced, have spent the intervening months in utterly misrepresentiog it, and fanning every spark of ignorant and selfish opposition into flame, inspired by the dark hope of wrecking a great measure which, with all its faults, will probably do more to lessen human misery than any other measure of our time." *

Mr. Harold Smith M.P. spoke in the House on Thursday, on the Tariff Reform amend- ment to the address. As usual, his assertions provoked a storm of contradictions, and a comment by Mr J. M. Robertson on the Member for Warrington's "amazing vindica- tion of the celebrated prophecies of Mr, Joseph Chamberlain." We are waiting for the occasion when Mr. Smith will no doubt take the opportunity of saying •before Mr, Lloyd George what he has often said in Warrington: that the land taxes cost .E 500,000 to collect and produced only £20,000. * • The Right Hon. F. E. Smith, M.P., does not usually hide his light under a bushel, but he surprised even some of his friends hi declaring in a recent speech "I am a Noncon- formist." Inquiry has shown that the brilliant Rupert of debate belongs to Wesleyan family at Birkenhead. Mr. Bona!' Law and Mr. Austen Chamberlain are also Nonconformists, so that the Anglican Churcb still has the prospect of having a number of its Bishops chosen for it by Congregs- tionalists, ,Presbyterians, Unitarians or Wesleyan Methodists.

_ Warrington Bridge. There is progress to report in connection with the Warrington Bridge works. Concrete and sheet piles are being driven to form the foundations of the river on the south-west side. The erection of coffer darn on each side of the river is also proceeding, the object being to keep the water from flooding the excavatione which will shortly begin. Pleturedro

What is the Alliance of Honour ? It is a company of youths and young men bandea together for the purpose of maintaining and exteudizift, amongt themselves and their fellows a high sense t° the advantages and obligations of purity of life. They desire to hold high the nobility and honoef of a life unsullied by impurity. Thel desire to save their fellows from the temptations t° which they are exposed— they believe that "a fence round the precipice at the top is better than a° ambulance at the bottom." This is not all—tbeY. desire to promote among young man a chivalrna° regard for the honour of woman, in fact, nothiag short of that brotherly concern and jealousy for the welfare of all young women which they cherish 00 behalf of their own sisters. And, above all thel desire that all who join their ranks may become the number who count it their greatest honour to 11‘' the servants of the Lord.

Mr. W. C. G. Gladstone, M.P. will address a Liberal meeting in the Co-operative Hall, on Friday evening, March 15th. Welsh Disestablishment. A protest meeting is announced to be held in the Parr Hall on Monday the 18th March, The Bishop of Liverpool is, we understand, to preside and speeches will be delivered by the Bishop of Llandaff, Mr. E. W. Johnson, a Welsh layman, of Llandudno and others. Warrington Photographic Society. Capt C. St John Broadbent on " Side Slips in Photography. " The return visit of Capt. Broadbent has been vividly anticipated by many members who had so greatly enjoyed his humorous sallies last April, and he in no way disapointed them on Tuesday. Much of his humour was quite new, treating with other sides of the art and practice, and many valuable hints were more forcibly driven home than they would have been in a formal and serious lecture. Mr. G. 11. Walker, was in the chair; and the v.,te of thanks, proposed by Mr. K F Bishop, and seconded by Mr. Rowlands, was heartily carried by the members (between 20 and 30) who were present. Death of Mr. Edward Wilson. Many of our readers will greatly regret to hear of the death of Mr. Edward Wilson, 73 Folly L toe, who passed away on the 16th inst. Mr. Wilson, who was a son of the late Mr. and Mrs. Wilson, of Clay Cross, Derbyshire, was 40 years of age. He had for ten years been associated with the Liberal Club and was a ominent member of the committee. He always took an active part in all that concerned the progress of the club and was much esteemed by his fellow members. Mr. Wilson, who was unmarried, had been in a delicate state of health for some time, but the end came unexpectedly. He was returning home from his work at Longford on Friday evening when he suddenly collapsed. He was assisted into a neigh- ouring house and was subsequently taken home but died the same evening The funeral took place at the Southern Cemetery, Didsbury, on Tuesday in the presence of many relatives and friends. The mourners were ; Mr and Mrs F P McNab, Manchester (sister) Mr W Wilson, (Merton (brother) Mr II Wilson,Wolver- hampton (brother) Miss Wilson and Mr Richard Wilson (sister and brother) Mr and Mrs Al Sturdy Manchester (sister) and Mr Arthur Woods. Mr A 'l' Clay (manager) represented the committee of the War- rington Liberal Club. Amongst the Warrington friends of the deceased who attended were, Messrs Frank Plurnpton G Baker and G Shaw. Wreaths were sent by the following : —Mr and Mrs McNab and family, Mr and Mrs H Wilson Miss Wilson and Mr it Wilson Mr and Mrs Sturdy. The Misses Wilson (neices) Ada Mabel and Edgar (nieces and nephew), Mr and Mrs John McNab Mr Mrs and Miss Woods, Mr and Mrs Ed Corksow, " His fellowworkmen " and" members of the Liberal Club."

The Examiner, Saturday,

WARRINGTON AND THE; COAL

STRIKE_

To an industrial centre like Warrington the possibility of a national coal strike offers an alarming prospect for, needless to say, the many manufactures upon which the inhabitants depend for a livelihood would soon come to a standstill in the event of a failure of the fuel supply. The situation is viewed with grave apprehension and all classes of the com- munity are fervently hoping for a peaceful settlement as the result of the timely Government intervention. At the great iron works of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., where about 2,000 men are employed, no intimation has yet been made to the lhands of any intention to close down but a repre- sentative of the " Examiner" was informed that a coal strike would undoubtedly mean a cessation of work. The company are, of course, the proprietors of large collieries, but no information was forthcoming as to ' how long the coal at present available is likely to last Messrs. Bylands Bros., Ltd., wire manufacturers Church-street, have already given their workpeople notice that, pending a battlement, they must consider themselves employed only from day to c:ay. The firm has, however, a fairly large supply of fuel in hand. Inquiries at several other large works, includ- ing Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sens, Ltd., and Monks, Hall and CJ., Ltd. showed that n 1 decisive steps had yet been taken, Ltd., firms as yet quietly awaiting tie course of events anl hoping for the best. The unrest in the coal world has been vividly brought home to the housewife by a much increased demand up m the household exchequer. Retail prices in Warrington have now reached Is. Id and Is. 2d. per cwt. as cmpired with 101 and Ild, in normal tunes. Since Sly.embar last there have been three advances, totalling about ss. per ton. The represen- tative of a prominent fi,m of coal merchants infermed our reporter that people were storing uncommonly large stocks of coal—in fact, there was a difficulty in coping with increased dement

LENTEN SERVICES.

THE BISHOP OF LIVERPOOL ON HOME LIFE.

The first of a series of Lenten services, arranged by the Warrington Church Society, was held in the Parish Church yesterday afternoon, when the Bishop of Liverpool delivered an address on "A Woman's Kingdom," to a large congregation of women. His lordship said they heard a lot to-day of women's rights. He wanted to remind them all that the kingdom over which a woman had the indisputable command was the home. There she reigned supreme. The home was a unit in the nation. The King had reminded them more than once that the advancement of a nation's greatness started in the homes of the people. Men might build houses but the Lomes were built by women. The mother was the great character-maker of the children in every home. She set an example and the children insensibly and naturally copied it. The Bishop touched upon the importance of discipline in the moulding of character. They should correct their children when they were in the wrong. As he passed down the streets of Liverpool at night he saw there after dark the surging crowd of young people, girls and boys scarcely out of their teens. The Head Constable of Liverpool had told him that they did not all come from bad homes but many from respectable homes. Their parents had, however, no influence whatever over them. They had been allowed to have all their own way. What wonder that a humanity of a most awful kind was gaining ground in our midst because fathers and mothers had failed to control their children in their younger days. Having expressed his hatred of "detestable pictures and jests which represented married life as all quarrel- someness and discord," the Bishop gave the following advice to wives :—(1) Never let your husband and yourself get angry together. If he is angry keep your temper, and if you get angry tell him to keep his. (2) Pray for your husband, and if possible with him. (3) Carry your religion into your daily life. Do not keep it for Sunday only. Do not be always preaching at your husband. He will not stand that any more than you would always stand him preaching at you. (4) Be natural and make your lives happy. In the evening the Bishop preached to men only and based his discourse on What we can do for our country." He said they were at the beginning of what must be a momentous year when great questions were before them in the political industrial, social, and Church Worlds. They could help our country by prayer. God loved to beat the people plead for their country, and God answered the prayers of a united people. When they were face to face with the great coal strike, face to face with great changes in I Church and State, if they could only draw near to God in prayer and plead on behalf of our country, they could not doubt that He would bear them. They could help England by their prayers, and also help England by their lives, by acknowledging God in all their ways and by putting God first.

Bewsey-road Wesley Guild. On Monday evening the young ladies in connection with the above Guild gave a very good entertainment, the proceeds of which were in aid of fonds for the forthcoming sale of work. The Guild room was packed by an appreciative audience. Mrs. Ferguson presided and Miss Healey opened with prayer. Miss Plinston, A.L.C.M., was the able accompanist and under her instruction the ladies' choir acquitted themselves admirably in a cantata entitled "The Golden Apple." Miss Millie Jones sang "Because "very sweetly and her light and breezy manner pleased everyone. Miss Winstanley is a young vocalist of great promise and her rendering of "A May morning" earned much applause. Miss Line Plinston who is always a welcome figure on the concert platform, sang with a very telling effect "The Enchantress and was loudly applauded, and for an encore she sang "By the fountain' in an effective manner. Mesdames Mills and Barlow were responsible for a good deal of laughter in their Lancashire recita- tions and resulted in encores being the rule. After an interval for refreshments the second half of the pro- gramme was filled in by the choir and members above mentioned. A pianoforte duet was ably rendered by Misses Walker and Wardle and a duet entitled "In the dusk of twilight" was sung by Misses Plinston and Jones very harmoniously. Miss Skelland proposed a vote of thanks, and spoke a few words of encourage- ment to the down-hearted young men whose concert the previous week was not as big a success as this, the adies', and in a few well chosen words she presented o Miss Plinston a present from the Guild Ladies' choir s a slight recognition of her valuable services. Miss Plinston suitably replied. Mrs. Corbett pronounced the Benediction and a very enjoyable evening was thus brought to a close.

MR. P. M. OLIVER

REPLIES TO TORY PAMPHLETS.

"THE LAND THAT GROWS THE CHILDREN'S BREAD."

Continuing his tour of the Knutsford Division, Mr P M Oliver, prospective Liberal candidate, addressed a meeting in the Independeut Methodist chapel,Grappen hall, on Thursday evening. Councillor C Jackson Holmes presided over a good attendance. The Chairman said they were met under somewhat Peculiar circumstances. For once the two political parties appeared to beagreed upon one point, and that was that the session on which they were entering would be one of the most momentous that this country had ever seen. (Hear' hear.) Measures which were going to be introduced by the Government would be of a very far-reaching character and perhaps the one which would occupy chief attention was the Home Rule Bill. That seemed to be a bugbear to some people but, as far as he was concerned, he hid no fear whatever about it. (Hear, hear.) It had been his good fortune to spend a considerable amount of time in Ireland, both in the North and in the South, and he knew the country pretty well. He could assure them that nowhere was he received with greater courtesy or more kindness than in the South of Ireland, where Home Rulers were so much in the majority. It compared most favourably with the north in that respect. Some people were under the impression that the whole of Ulster was against Home Rule but, if they illiminated Belfast, there was not much anti-Home Rule feeling even in that province. (Applause). Mr Oliver first dealt with the Insurance Aet, with the special reference to the position of friendly societies. He pointed out that the Government could have insured the people by setting up a great State institution run by a lot of highly-salaried officials, but they decided to make use of the existing friendly societies,trade unions and industrial insurance companies.Ele claimed that the new Act would ensure that all friendly societies were perfectly sound before they came within the scheme and that no injury whatever would be done to the present members of those organizations. In Cheshire they had about 190 friendly societies with over 126,000 members. There were more members of ' friendly societies in Cheshire than in any other English agricultural county or in the whole of Scotland. (Applause.) The accumulated funds of the Cheshire societies amounted to £260,000 and it must be borne in mind that not one penny of that would be touched by the Insurance Act. The whole of those funds would be applied for the benefit of the members who had built them up. (Applause.) The amount worked out to an average of £2 per member, which meant that Cheshire friendly societies were in a worse position financially than those of any other county,with the exception of Worcestershire. The accumulated funds of the English friendly societies as a whole averaged £5 per member and in Suffolk the average was £l5 per member. It might be that many of the friendly societies at a time of great illness would find themselves in a very difficult position, but the Act came along and in the middle of this year it would make every friendly society in this country solvent. (Applause). Mr Oliver touched upon the superior benefits which societies would be able to confer upon the present members as the result of the contri- butions of the employer and the State, and made special reference to a scheme drawn tip by the Hearts of OA Society. He also showed that toe Act offered more generous terms with regard to arrears than the friendly societies were able to offer. He thanked God that. Mr Boner Law was not in power to repeal or drastically amend such a beneficent measure as the Insurance Act. Mr Oliver then addressed himself to a number of pamphlets circulated by the Conservatives in those parts of the division where the Liberals had been hold- ing meetings. The first took the form of a child's appeal which read : Don't tax the land that grows the children's bread, But tax the foreigner who takes dad's work instead. (Laughter.) When people wrote poetry one had to for- give them a great deal, because the first essential was that it must rnyme. (Laughter.) He wanted to know who was " taxing the land that grows the children's bread," because he thought everybody knew that under the Budget agricultural land was not taxed. (Applause.) It was a strange thing that a pamphlet like that should be issued by the Conservatives when they knew that the Conservative Party was out, not fcr taxing " the land which grows the children's bread," but for taxing the children's bread itself, which was a much more serious matter. (Applause.) The Liberals had not taxed " the land that grows the children's bread "—in fact if they looked into Imperial finance, they would find that dad and his children were paying less in taxation than they did under the Conservative Government. (Applause ) The only things that the Liberal Government had taxed which the working classes consumed were spirits and tobacco. Of coarse all the spirits were not drunk by the working classes sod all the tobacco was not smaked by them but assuming that they were, the Liberal Government had increased taxation on the workingclasses by £3,500,000. But they I had taken £3,700,000 off sugar and £1,200,000 off tea. (Applause). We had got old age pensions, a stronger navy, labour exchanges and a great deal of other good social work, and yet the working classes now were taxed a little less than they were under the Conservative Government. (Applause). Mr. Oliver finally dealt with some virulent pamphlets on Home Rule and pointed out that wherever we had granted Home Rile it had resulted in the clo ier attach- ment of the country in question to our Empire. He laid special stress on the examples of South Africa and Canada and concluded by quoting a resolution of the Senate of Australia declaring that in accordance with the most cherished tradition of British Government and British justice, and to cement the Empire into one harmonious whole, Home Rule should be granted to Ireland." (Applause.) Councillor Grindle, of Eccles, delivered a very inter- esting address on the Insurance Act.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. "Peggy." It has come to be an accepted belief that musical comedies are sure of a hearty welcome at Warrington. The combination of music and mirth, pretty girls and pretty frocks, seems to suit the taste of ninety-nine theatre-goers in a hundred. This season has been an exceptional one from a Warrington point of "view, and thanks are due to Mr. Loader for the enterprising way he has catered for his patrons. The current week sees the popular theatre occupied by " Peggy," one of Mr. George Dance's latest London successes, with the book and a leading role by 'Mr.George Grossnaith, ,jun. Candidly speaking, we do not think " Peggy " as quite in the same class as some of the other "girls" —"The Quaker Girl," for instance —who have visited us in the guise of musical comedy. There is less than usual romance in her story, and her adventures fall far short of beinz thrilling, hut she and her friends never ;fail to amuse. We first, make their acquaintance in the lounge of the New Hotel, London. Here Peggy Barrison practises her profession as a manicurist, largely on the gilded youth of the metropolis. She also contracts a somewhat unnecessary engagement with the hotel hairdresser, one Albert Umhles. The lounge is the scene of some very comical displays of manicure, hair-dressing and love making. Then the advent of a millionaire or two results in the translation of the whole party to Friville, a very Frenchy watering place. The dis- covery of Mr. Umbles' real long-lost uncle, and the unmasking of the sham uncle, both take place on " the plage " near the fruit and flower stall, and all ends " merry and bright." In original musical numbers which are likely to make any impression " Peggy" is notably lacking, and the chief aim of almost all concerned in the production is to tickle the risibilities. There is plenty to laugh at in the idiosyncracies of Albert Umbles, the pleasantly fraudulent Grossmith-like character of Anberon Blow, and the susceptibility of the lion. James Bendoyle to the tender passion. Messrs. Vincent Erne,Field Fisher, and Jack Morrison all find congenial roles in these parts respectively. Miss Ruth Trafford sings and dances charmingly as "Peggy," and an equal success is gained by Miss Ruby Vyvyan as Daris Bartle. Miss Gertrude Aylward revives pleasant recollections of her triumphs in "The Arcadians" by her rich comedy as Lady Snoop, and Miss• Dorothy Whittaker captivates all in the sprightly role of Polly Polino of the Alcazar stage, Friville. The two scenes are beautifully mounted, and the whole production goes with a merry swing that ensures its general success as a ugh entertainment.

M. Loader, as a memento of the most unique event in the history of the St. Helens Theatre, The Golden Land of Fairy Tales,' played by invitation of the Mayor to 12,000 school children of the Borough of St. Helens, February 1912. FREE TRADE TRIUMPHANT. THE CYCLE TRADE. [BY C. E. PARKER] Every manufactured article entering a " protected " country is challenged at the Custom House and made dearer by the amount of the duty. Every manufactured article entering a Free Trade country is challenged by our home manufacturers. Which challenge is more affective?. Which best for the country?. Wait and see. Under Protection the price of imported manufactures plus the duty helps to deter- mine the level of price for the competing home maker. He aims at putting his pro- duct on the market slightly below the im- ported taxed article. Under Free Trade the home manufacturer has to beat the foreign import at the foreigner's price without eny added duty

Can he do it?. Yes.—unless the foreign market has some distinct natural advantage. Why?. Because he has the advantage of free im- ports affecting every single item that enters into his cost of manufacture. Does he do it?. Think and see. Take the trades brought into prominence at the inception of Tariff Reform. _ . _

Wool manufactures " Threatened" in 1902. In 1911 up in exports more than 100 per cent, imports down 20 per cent.

. Gone up 50 per

The Iron Trade" going" cent in exports. What about glass? Earthenware and Glass up 50 per cent. Cotton "Cotton will go, "has gone-50 per cent up! Take some of the trades that did not receive honourable mention. Boots were dealt with a few weeks ago. Take the Cycle Trade. The exports of this trade are only given separately from 1905. Here are numbers exported for every one imported In 1905 for every 1 bought we sold 25 1906 „ 1 Of 72 177 169

1907 „ 1 1908 „ 1 If

360

1910 1 , 9 _

„ 1909 1 $, 239 360 474

1911

In the imports we have the figures back to 1902. The net retained imports in that year were 10,214 ; they have steadily sunk until in 1911 we only imported 309 cycles. From America alone in 1902 we imported gross 12,551 ; only 16 in 1910. A great many British made cycles have passed over Warrington Bridge since 1902. In 1902 Tariff Reformers raised an outcry for Protection against the 12,000 American cycles imported without any Custom House challenge. The challenge of the manufacturer has been effective, and reduced these imports practically to nil, increased the proportion of exports to imports, until they now stand at 471 to 1, or to express it in full figures, Exports 1911 146,718, Imports 309. The total American exports have gone down to about one-sixth of what they were in 1902, British exports have gone up probably about 500 per cent. Is it conceivable that cycle manufac- turers could have increased their exports so raptdly under a Protective system? You cannot have Protection for one trade; if the cyclemaker gets protection against imported cycles, other trades must have the equiva- lent for what they make, and this would influence all the cycle maker buys. It is the help of cheap production, the result of our Free Trade system, which has enabled our cycle makers not only to beat such countries as America in our own market, but also in the markets of the world.

Under Free Trade industries exposed to foreign competition pass through a series of stages.

First Stage.--Characteristics : increasing imports of manufactured articles competing with the home make. A general outcry for a protective tariff. A newspaper propaganda carried on by papers of the Mail and Dispatch type. Tariff Reform leaflets galore. Dump- ing Exhibitions, the imported articles on show, doleful speeches about British Trade about the wicked foreigner who "tramples" upon us and we wont longer 'take it lying down' Sze. Union Jacks waved and worn.

All the time the alert manufacturer is quietly challenging the imported article in the right place. Copying what is worth copying, improving his machinery,increasing his plant to reduce costs, and developing efficlency in every department. Second Stage.—As a result of this increased efficiency the home market is recaptured and the tide of imports checked'. Third Stage.—With the home market re-captured and supplied, the manufacturer turns to the export market and with efficiency and cheap production, the result of Free Trade, developes a growing oversee trade, providing labour for Britishers and rebuilding such towns as Coventry. Mean- while the Tariff Reformers drop the cycle trade from the number of their "injured " innocents, the figures and facts being too hot, and seek out some other "injured industry," to take the place of the vacated cycle leaflet, —some industry passing through the First Stage of development. Cycles no longer appear at Dumping Exhibitions, gravestones and hops take their place. Only gravestones apply to Warrington, hops absent. • Which challenge is the more effective, that of the Customs or the manufacturers? Surely the challenge of the manufacturers. But there is another side to the question more important than that of manufacturing. The two, however, cannot well be separated, the action upon each other being reciproca- tory. I speak of the maker and the user. It should ever be remembered that We manu- facture to satisfy human wants. Working, manufacturing, are only the means not the end. We do not make cycles for" the joy of the working " but for the use of the rider, for hiswork or pleasure.`How does the matter stand from his point of view, and how does his point of view affect the nation's well being? The averge price of cycles in 1903 was based on the only available figures those of export. £6 10s., in 1911 X 4 each, a difference of £2 10s. The difference may not all be the result of cheapened production, some portion of the value may have been taken out of the cycle. This may be so and a depreciation, shall we say of 20 per cent. of the saving allowed. This still leaves us with a balance of £2, the result of efficiency and reduction of cost. The output of British cycles may be roughly taken as 650,000 per annum, which at £2 a cycle gives £1,300,000 set at liberty, and retained by the cycle buyer to spend on some article satisfying some other want, requiring the labour of others. Herein we see how Free Trade calls for additional labour. It is triumphant all along the line. Better for the manufacturer, better for the user, better for the worker and better for the country. it may be allowed that even with a 10 per cent. tariff on cycles, efficiency in manu- facture to some extent would have developed but the forces that call this forth would not have been so potent, because with the elimination of the foreign competition, one great spur would have been removed. It would then have been possible for cycle manufacturers instead of seeking the profit through more efficient production to have gained it by an arrangement of prices.

GRAPPENHALL.

• Costly Sewerage Scheme OPPOSITION FROM RATEPAYER'S MEETING LIVELY SCENE.

Ratepayers of G-raiMe-n-ha-11-a-se-e-MTiled in strong force at a parish meeting held in the National schools on Tuesday ~evening, to record their protest against a sewerage scheme drawn up by the Runcorn Rural District Council for the parishes of Gmppenhall, Latchford Without and Thelwall. The undertaking is estimated to cost £18,500 which will increase the rates of Grappenhad to the extent of at least is. 21d. in the £. Great interest and not a little indignation has been aroused by the rural authority's determination to proceed with such a costly scheme, and at the rate- payers' meeting a unanimous resolution of protest was carried. • Mr. F R Roberts (chairman of the Parish Council) who presided, explained the reasons for the absence of the clerk Mr C H Hoult, who he said was indisposed, and Mr. Burrows, their representative on the Rural District Council, who had written stating that he was confined to bed with a sprained knee. (A voice : " Shame.") Accidents, remarked the Chairman, could not be helped by them. A fortnight ago, he continued the three councils concerned met Mr Diggle, the engineer to the proposed scheme, who explained to them that the cost would be £18,500, which would involve a rate of Is. 2A. in the £. The drainage of the three parishes would he taken up to Thelwall and there dealt with by a process of filtration. A great deal was said at the meeting as to the tardy way in which the District Council had acted. They had good ground for com- plaint that the parishes were not consulted before. (Hear, hear.) They had to pay the piper, and it was only right that they should have a voice in calling the tune. The Rural District Council never asked them whether they thought the scheme was necessary, but brought it before them cut and dried. He must admit that it seemed to be a very good scheme, but it was very expensive. All that the Parish Council could do was to raise objections at the Local Government Board inquiry ; beyond that they could not go. If the Rural District Council succeeded in satisfying the Local Government Board, the scheme would go through and the ratepayers would have to pay for it. He contended that although the procedure of the District Council might be legal, it was hardly courteous. Mr. N. Cook: Have the Runcorn Rural District [Council passed this scheme ?

The 0.1-airman : Certainly. Mr. Nicholls said each of the parishes was repre- sented on the Oistrict Council. How was it they had not known about the matter before? He contended that the Grappenhall member had been somewhat at fault in not keeping the parish better posted up on the scheme.._

Death of Professor Young.

The death occurred on Thursday, at his residence Raynham-avenue, Didsbury, of Professor A. H. Young, L.L.D., F.R.C.S., formerly Professor of Anatomy in the Victoria University, Manchester (son of Mr. J. C. Young, Palmyra-square, Warrington, and brother of Mr. J. Rymer Young, J.P. Professor Young was a native of Warrington, where he was born in 1852. He received his education at University College, Liverpool, and Edinburgh University, where he graduated MB,, CM., in 1876. He went to Manchester,and was appointed demonstra- tor and assistant lecturer •in anatomy in the Owens College, under Professor Morrison , Watson. In 1881 he became lecturer in surgical pathology in the Owens College. and in 1885 he succeeded to the Chair of Pathology, while he was also appointed Dean of the Medical School. He resigned the Chair of Anatomy in 1906, in consequence of failing health. Professor Young was a strenuous opponent of the introduction of women students into the School of Medicine.

Gravel? Dropsical Swellings? Rheumatism?

Kidney and Bladder Trouble is Serious.

Mr. E. Oakley, of 1, Bond-street,Sankey Bridges, Warrington, says:—" I can truthfully say that Doan's pills have done me a lot of good, and I hive recommended them to my mates. " I used to be a sufferer from dropsy and kidney trouble ; my limbs swelled very much. I was subject to severe pains in the small of my back, hips and loins. a-SOC/A,

Emmanuel (Reformed) Church.

The Sunday school teaparty was held in the Institute on February 16th, when a large number of scholars sat down to tea. At 7.30 p.m. an excellent entertainment was provided by the children, to the great appreciation of the parents of the scholars. This was followed by the distribution of prizes. The superintendent (Mr T G Moon) and the secretary (Mr A Johnson) both pre- sented encouraging reports. The number of scholars had not greatly increased, but there was marked progress in efficiency. There were two very hopeful features—more scholars attended morning school and Divine Service, and a higher proficiency in the Scriptures was noticeable, due in no small measure to the • Bible Searches " given week by week. It was stated that instruction is given in the Church Creeds, and services on Evangelical lines. After the reports had been presented, the Rev. W. T. Jaye (curate-in- charge) said he desired in the presence of the parents of the scholars to tender his heartfelt thanks to the officers and teachers of the Sunday School. Ile realised that it was a branch of the Church which demanded the best efforts of the best workers obtainable. He counted himself happy to have the services of so loyal a staff of teachers. The prizes were distributed by Mrs. W. T. Jaye.

===== 0002601_19120224_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
Bold-street Wesleyan Church.

Visit of the Rev. F. W. Macdonald.

Special services were held in connection with the Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday, when the Rev W Macdonald, of London occupied the pulpit and preached two eloquent sermons. Great interest was taken in the rev gentleman's visit and there were large congregations. In the morning the choir rendered the anthem " I waited for the Lord " in a creditable manner, Mrs Booth and Miss Emily Taylor being the soloists. The Rev F W Macdonald, iu dealing with the subject of public worship,said that the old Psalmist had a joy in the public worship of God. Ile entered His House, not with lagard step, not merely as a custom or habits, but with a bearing of thankfulness and hope. How far was it so now ? Those who were watching the tendencies of the present day were saying that public worship was declining, and that it had lost its hold on the general mind and slipped out of the pro- gramme of general life. For Israel the presence of God was localised in the temple. Nowadays we did not possess that localised presence in the same sense, but, they had Christ's promise that" Where two or three are gathered together in my Name, there am I in the midst of them." The neglecting of worship told disastrously on those who were guilty of it. Every clan of life needed nourishment. The nourish- ment for the human being was the Word of God. For in many things one could always find a substitute, but there was no substitute for the worship of God. Literature was no substitute for worship and they could not be exchanged one for the other. What satisfied the natural could not satisfy the spiritual life. All life must, have the nurture that belonged to it. A man could not live solely on amusements ; his soul needed the nurture of the word of God which he got in public worship. The form of wrong-doings might differ but from London to the ends of the earth, greed, cruelty, injustice, selfishness worked out innumer- able forms of wickedness of which the soul ought to be rid. There was a kind of brotherhood existing among all people whether they recognized it or not. They all belonged to one heritage and everyone ought to feel some sense of kinship. We worked, played and traded together and we must worship together, for it was in worship that tee deepest elements in our common lot were revealed. Worship was not complete BO long as it was merely individual and limited ; they must all join together. Every class should be united. They should all be one family because they had all one Father. One should challenge another to come into His courts. The greatest harm human beiugs suffered was the harm inflicted by themselves. , No man could take another away from God, but a man could do it himself if he was fool enough. They wanted unity and they should all join together as one body in the worship of God. In the evening the rev. gentlemsn delivered another eloquent sermon, taking for his subject the power and value of the Holy Scriptures. The choir rendered the anthem "The Wilderness," Mr. E. H. Pearce and Mr. S. Hassell were the soloists.

===== 0002601_19120224_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
St Mary's, Buttermarket. Guild of the Sacred Heart.

A meeting was held in connection with the above on Sunday, February 12th, under the chairmanship of the Rev. N. Wilson, ss hen Mr. George Milligan of Liver- pool (author of " Life through Labour's eyes ") gave an address on "The Catholic Church and Labour." Mr. Milligan outlined the Catholic workers' policy as contrasted with that of the Socialists. This policy, he ssid, had been clearly detailed by Pope Leo XIII. in his wonderful encyclicals, and he exhorted all Catholics to study those encyclicals, and, as far as possible, to teach them. He regarded the Trade Guilds of the Middle Ages as the patterns to be set up and copied. Thorolcl Rogers, the great student of social reform, has said, that under these Guilds the workingman enjoyed his golden age, whilst Mr. Hyndmau, the Father of English Socialism, has said that it would be a good thing if we could return to the conditions which then obtained. Mr. Milligan pointed out that the success of these Guilds was due to the fact that they were founded on religion. The Socialists of to-day, were, be said, too short-sighted, they looked downward instead of upward. In conclusion he asked all Catholics to take an earnest interest in this great question of Social Reform and to become, as Leo XIII. had asked them, Labour Crusaders—Modern Defenders of the Faith. Mr. Milligan was thanked for his address, on the proposition of Mr. S. J. Price, seconded by Mr. T. Dolan.

Lenten Services.

11111111n4 4

===== 0002601_19120224_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
REL/GlOl/.

Lent began on Wednesday when there were numerous services in the Anglican and Roman Catholic Churches. The period is one of exceptional activity in Church life and in addition to the usual parochial observances, the Church Society has arranged a series of lectures for women and men each Friday afternoon and evening. Lent is commonly said to be from a Saxon word for Spring. It was originally called Quadragesims, and only lasted forty hours—from 7.2 on Good Friday to Easter morning, but it was gradually extended to forty days, after the fasts of Moses, Elijah, the Nine rites and Our Lord Himself, all of whom fasted forty days. The fast begins on Ash Wednesday, because the six Sundays, being festivals, were not included in the fast- ing days; and therefore, unless four days were added before the first Sunday in Lent, the fast would only last thirty-six days instead of forty. Ash Wednesday —the first day of Lent—originated in the blessing of ashes on that day, "to put in remembrance every Christian man, the beginning of Lent and penance, that he is but ashes and earth, and thereunto shall return " ; and the ceremony was reserved at the Reformation.

===== 0002601_19120224_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Dedication of Memorials at Croft Church.

At Christ Church, Croft, on Saturday afternoon the Lord Bishop of Liverpool dedicated the reredos, altar rails and croon panelling, in memory of Mary Wadeson, and two stained lancet windows in memory of James and Mary Hard iker. There was a large congregation at the special service which was most impressive. The following clergymen were present : Rev Canon Willis Rev W Bracecamp (Warrington), Rev J T Owen (Winwick), Rev E W W Kaye (Newchurch), and the Rector of Croft. After special psalms and lessons, the Rector (Rev H I Wadeson) requested the Lord Bishop to dedicate the reredos anti other gifts, and the Chancellor's faculty for the same having been read, the Bishop dedicated the various gifts as follows :—"ln the faith of Jesus Christ, I dedicate this reredos and other gifts to the glory of God, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost. Amen." The hymn "Hark ! the sound of holy voices," having been sung, the Lord Bishop gave an address from the words "That ye be not slothful, but followers of them who, through faith and patience, inherit the promises."

===== 0002601_19120224_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
Lancashire Cleric and Disestabllshment. Convinced it Would be a Blessing.

Canon Green, of Manchester. in Wednesday's" Stan- dard, " says:—Though I am following the campaign against the Disestablishment of the Church in Wales with interest. Ido not find myself able to take any part in it. I have for a long time been convinced that disestablishment not merely of the Church in Wales, but of the whole Church of England, would be a blessing to the Church and to national religion. When I think of the Erastianism, which seems to me a necessary result of establishment, the impotence of the Church in the choice of her own bishops, the almost complete silencing of the laity . . . I feel that the disestsblishment and the most drastic measure of disendowment would be a small price to pay for the liberty and for the right to lean on nothing but the love and loyalty of the Church's own members. 1 recognise that disendowment is a seperate matter. But here, without pretending to be a financial or historical expert, I must say that justice seems to de- mand some measure of disendowmeut . . .

All Saints' Mission.

The annual social gathering was held in the mission room, Hoyle-street, on Monday night, when a record number sat down to an excellent tea which reflected great credit upon the organiser, Mrs Bankes, wife of the curate-in-cbarge. ===== 0002601_19120224_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
Revising the Prayer Book. Canon Morley Stevenson one of the Committee.

----- Last week the Archbishop of Canterbury announced at Westminster the names of the liturgical experts who have accepted his invitation to serve upon the com- mittee for the forthcoming Revision of the Prayer Book. The list is a comprehensive one, and likely to be hailed with satisfaction by Churchmen of all schools Of thought. It was Dr. Gore who first suggested the inclusion of expert liturgiologists upon the Revision Committee, one the Archbishop of Canterbury is to be congratulated no less upon his discrimination in the choice of experts to whom the invitation was to be sent than upon the eminently satisfactory body of trusted scholari, who have found themselves able to accept that invitation. Whatever the outcome of their delibera- tions, it is safe to say that their conclusions will have immense weight with all parties in the Church.— In Liverpool (says a special correspondent of the " Liverpooi Poet end Mercury ") we all rejoice to see the names of Canon Morley Stevenson and Dr. Guy Warman upon the selected committee. Both are scholars of repute and of sound judgment, and both enjoy to a remarkable degree the confidence of Church- men whose doctrinal standpoint differs from their own no less than of those of their own school. Canon Morley Stevenson is one of the most finished and one of the most scholarly preachers in the diocese, and, probably—with the single exception of Prebendary Wakeford—is at the present moment the greatest "draw." Quite recently he has edited, in company with Canon Buford and the Rev. J. W. 'ryrer, the new " Dictionary of the Prayer Book," soon to be published by Messrs. Pitman, and this editoral work has brought him into close contact with the latest results of modern liturgical scholarship. His invitation to join the Committee of Revision is peculiarly fitting, and is also a matter of gratification to his many friends in 1 the diocese of Liverpool and beyond it.

===== 0002601_19120224_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
Archbishop Whiteside's Lenten Pastoral. Prevalence of " Sacrilegious Confessions."

Archbishop Whiteside, in his Lenton Pastoral, read in all the churches of the Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Liverpool on Sunday,comments on present day evils, lamenting that masses of men and women have no con- cern as to the duties as creatures they owe to their Maker. When passion, unrestrained by fear of God, dominates man's heart it is not to be wondered that innumerable evils should spring up—a prelude to eternal evils in the world to come. " Hence ' says the Archbishop," the battle in almost every country rages between God and Satan around the school, and the victory is to the one who controls in the school the moral training of the future citizens of a country. " Ills Grace Bays : "Catholics have reason to be thankful that in our land, in our own schools, the fruits of so many sacrifices, Catholic children are being thoroughly instructed in their religion. Moreover, the adult Catholic population, generally speaking, gladly listen to the word of God, which is assiduously preached to them, and thus the religious knowledge of earlier years , is revised and extended." After emphasising the necessity of prayer, his Grace proceeds to lay stress on the Sacrament of Penance, accompanied by contrition and satisfaction. Ills Grace remarks that experienced confessors deplore the large number of sacrilegious confessions, sacrilegious not because sins are deliberately concealed, but becanse there is not true contrition for Bin. Special stress is laid on the importance of this consideration by the catechism. The motives themselves of true sorrow are familiar owing to their being incorporated in the act of contrition recited by all. They are, the punish- rnents which grievous sin has deserved in the next world, the fact that the sinner, as St. Paul says, is guilty of "crucifying again the Son of God, and making Him a mockery, ' and lastly, that it is an offence to God Who is Bo good in Himself, and so good to us*

===== 0002601_19120224_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
St. John's Presbyterian Church.

On Sunday evening the Rev. W. Reid spoke on the disruption in the Israelitish kingdom at the death of Solomon brought about by the uprising of the Northern tribes. He attributed this to a threefold cause, viz., the despotism and weakness of Solomon, the insolent folly of Rehoboam, and the intriguing ambition of Jeroboam. Speaking of the shattered dream of an Empire, he yet showed how, viewing the position from the Divine aspect, in the very heart of the national catastrophe a new opportunity was given, to the northerners in their newly found freedom, and in that presented to the southerners to strip their religion of its heathenish elements, for both to see and return the vision of their Divine mission.

A HEALTH WEEK.

Novel Proposal to Popularise Sanitary Reform

"It is safe to say that more people die in one year from ignorance than from any other single cause— ignorance of the laws of health, ignorance of the causes and prevention of disease, ignorance of housecraft, ignorance of all that appertains to the birth and bring- ing up of children." Thus Mr Arthur J Martin, M. Inst., C.E.P.U.S., President of the Institute Of Sanitary Engineers, in an address at a meeting held at the Town liall,Warrington, on Tuesday afternoon to consider a novel proposal for the popularization of measures for the protection and improvement of the public health. It will take the form of a national health week, in which over 100 important towns are preparing to take part, with the object of arousing an intelligent interest in health among all members of the community. The week I selected is from April 28th—May 4th. Sermons will be preached in churches and chapels. In the schools short and simple addresses will be given on pure sir and cleanliness and kindred subjects. The leading picture palaces in many towns have arranged to show interest- ing films illustrative of some health topic. Visits will be arranged to some of the public works connected ' with the health administration of the town.

The meeting called by the Mayor of Warrington to discuss the subject attracted a fairly largo and repre- sentative attendance. His Worship presided and amongst those present were :—Alderman James Evans (chairman of the Health Committee),Alderman Bennett, the Rectbr (Rev Canon Willis), Revs Canon Morley Stevenson and G Vale Owen, Dr Hutt (assistant medical officer of health), Mr Wilfrid .1 Rowland (secretary of the Citizens' Guild of Help). The MAYOR confessed that when the matter was first brought under his notice he did not think much of it because he believed the Health Committee were doing their best to improve the health of the community— (hear, hear)—and he thought anything else was super- fluous. He brought the question under the notice of the Health Committee and they did not seem to be captivated very much, but on further consideration it was thought the matter was well worth a trial. The suggestion if carried out might do a great amount of good and any experiment which was calculated to improve the moral and physical tone of the people it was his duty as Mayor to encourage. (Applause). Mr. A. J. MARTIN, who represents the Agenda Club by which the national health week is being organized, devoted a striking address to the question of ignorance on health questions. He said there had been an impression that the race as a whole was deteriorating. That, however, was not borne out by any reliable evidence, but there was undoubtedly a deterioration which took place in the lite of the individual. Four- fifths of our children, in spite of the adverse circum- stances attending their birth, were born with healthy constitutions, but how many preserved them? Many of us were in the habit of looking upon physical deterioration as the result of poverty, and no, in a measure, it was. But that was only part of the truth. The sting of poverty was ignorance—that pitiful Ignorance of nutrition which squandered the slender Income on foods that did not feed and which sapped the strength and efficiency of the breadwinner by chronic I indigestion. Ignorance was as prevalent in the ranks of the well-to-do as it was among the poor, and there was one manifestation of it which threatened the very foundations of our social systern—he referred to the lack of knowledge on the part of the comfortable classes of the conditions under which the poor existed. It was not a matter of humanity only,but of self-preserva- tion. We could not harbour, a plague spot in onr midst and escape contagion. The evil effects of dirty, verminous environment could not he confined to those who actually lived in it. Not only diagnorance open the door to disease, but it banged, barred and bolted the door in the face of every sanitary reform. The only effective remedy for ignorance was education, but we could not begin to educate people until we had got them to realise , that there was something which it concerned them to I know. Our great grandchildren would marvel at the apathy of a generation which was content to put up with measles, tuberculosis and typhoid fever I as part of the natural order of things. Nor were our descendants likely to view with approval that spurious refinement which found its chief expression in shutting our eyes to unpleasant facts and which led us to tolerate any evil, whether social, moral or hygienic, which did not absolutely force itself upon our notice. It was estimated that in London alone there were 200,000 school children suffering from defects largely preventable. We lavished money on hospitals for the sake of performing a small proportion of costly cures while a little care would hive prevented the evil altogether. (Hear, hear and applause). Surely one week in the year should be set aside in which the public health should be the chief concern of every man and woman in the country who took any interest in public affairs. How much time was recently given up to a campaign of bickering, insinua- tion, recrimination and slander, all for the purpose ol clouding and confusing a Constitutional issue ? How much newspaper space had been devoted to the nauseous details of one miserable crime—the Crippen case? Waa the health of the people of so little import- ance in comparison that one week in the year was too much to give up to it? This was a cause which meant more for the well-being of the country than the pro- grammes of all political parties rolled into one, and they had, therefore, a right to claim a share of public attention for it. (Applause). On the proposition of Mr B S TOWNROE, seconded I by Mr Holman Kingdon, it was resolved :—" That this meeting cordially commends the idea of a national health week, and agrees to appoint a committee with power of co-option to initiate and supervise the local celebration of the week."

The RECTOR moved :—" That the clergy and ministers of Warrington and the neighbourhood be respectfully invited to preach on the subject of national health on Sunday, April 28th." He should be glad to do so himself and he hoped the other clergy and ministers would be willing to accede to the request. The Rev. Canon Srr,vms,ox seconded, and said the churches ought to be unanimous in doing their best to help the question forward in every possible way. (Applause). On the motion of Mr. ROWLAND, seconded by Mr. W. H. STANSFIELD, it was decided: "That the Health Committee be asked to co-operate in the celebration of the national health week in any way it seems fit to them, but with the special object of informing and educating the general public on the important work which is being done in their name." The following were appointed a provisional corn- mittee:—The Mayor,Alderman James Evans, Mr JMoore Murray, Canons Willis and Stevenson, Mr F A Frost, Mr E Plinston, Mr G Weaver, Mr E Wynne Jones, the Rev G Vale Owen, Miss M Broadbent, Mrs Pattison, Father Wilson, Mr B S Townroo, Mr Ransome, Mr Rowland, Mr Stansfield, the Rev W A Beckett, Mr C E Parker, a representative of the N.U.T. and as many members of the Health Committee as that committee decide to appoint.

I use this sub-headline with apologies to the shade of Dickens, and I promise that the chapter shall be short. It is suggested by what I lead in my " Examiner " of the meeting of tb6 Warrington Church Society and the Rector's address thereat. I came to it fresh from a gathering at which the pi incipal speaker was the Very Reverend Dean of St. A siph, the subject being the projected Disestablisment and Disendowment of of the Church in Wales'. I take the liberty of saying that in my humble judgment the pro- ceedings were not very well engineered. The only little anecdote which the Dean, although an exceedingly well-favoured and jolly-looking ecclesiastic, used to point his moral and adorn his tale, was not only con- fessedly 'rather vulgar, but a particularly ancient chestnut. The reader will immediately recognise it when I state that it had reference to the youngster who, invested by his economical mother in a pair of his dad's discarded trousers which had simply been shortened in the legs but not otherwise adapted to the anatomy of the protesting juvenile, objected that it was impossible for him in such ill-fitting garments to tell whether be were going to or returning from school, In explaining my bit of criticism respecting the indifferent stage management on the occasion, may I be a trifle, vulgar, too? There was a solicitor in the chair; nevertheless the promoters arranged that another solicitor should propose the leading resolution, and that a third solicitor should second this! I was somehow reminded af the Irish petty ' sessions usher who, on being directed to exclude the public (but not the members of the Bar) by reason of a sitting in camera btwled—" Orl ye blaggards wot izn't liars quit the Coort !" On the occasion to which I allude, three lawyers very eloquently and no doubt sincerely supported Mr. Dean and his plea for the maintenance of the Welsh Establishment; but it is just as well for me that as it passed on to record that fact, my pen had quite com- pletely got rid of the Hibernian accent as it were,and thus avoided misspelling the word "lawyers." A MODERN WHITTINGTON.

At the outset of my journalistic career in a York- shire town I was associated with a little chap called John Hitching. Four years my junior, he was—well, not to put too fine a point upon it, though of course employing the term in its familiar and strictly techni- cal and not in any offensive sense, he was the printers' devil. Within a few years he severed his connection with the place, and, after a cordial leave-taking and mutual good wishes respecting the future,tramped off to woo Dame Fortune the fickle, amid fresh woods and pastures new. He is now widely known as Sir Thomas Brooke-Hitching, a wealthy London merchant. As the " Times " pointed out only the other day, for 20 years he has been one of the representatives of his Ward in the Court of Commons Council • he served the office of Sheriff in 1902-3 ; is one of his Majesty's Lieutenants for the City of London, and has held numerous public appointments. He has served the office of Mayor of St. Marylebone, in which borough he resides, and for a time he represented the City of London on the London County Council. lie has con- tested seats in Parliament in the Conservative interest, but without success. He is, in business, the head of Hitclaings (Limited), makers of perambu- lators and baby-care. He has been Master of the Makers of Playing Cards Company. Last week this old pal of mine—who has never forgotten the companions of his boyhood was elected Alderman for the ward of Farriagdon Within, being preferred before Mr. John Walter of the " Times," by a majority of 31 votes. And here am I, after a strenuous thirty to fotty years' interval, atill--but, then I made the mistake of remaining in newspaperdom, where few there be who make their pile and a decided minority succeed in "striking ile ' whereas Master Tom Hitching" as was "early washed his hands of printers' ink, turned his back on a journalism that has more kicks than lia'pence for lots of its devotees, and, with the vaulting ambition that in his case has not o'er-leaped itself, bounded to fame and fortune with the aid of domestic perambulators and simple baby cars, enjoying royal and aristocratic patronage. Moreover, any reader who may happen to be interested in my narrative may take from me the assurance that Sir Thomas is not even yet at the summit of his legitimate desires.; such, at all events is my conviction, and I confidently predict that, given the continued robust health which I trust may be hie, he will not rest satisfied with less than, and in due course will triumphantly achieve, the Lord Mayoralty itself. The Lord Mayor being selected from Alder- men who have been sheriffs, the choice is restricted within a comparatively simple circle, so that the chances of this once rioters' boy of humble parentage and lowly station (whose earn, in childhood, my wife playfully boxed more than once) are not at all remote. Just fancy! Dick Whittington the second!

HOW TO TREAT PIMPLES AND BLiCKHEADS.

To treat disfiguring pimples and blackheads, gently smear the affected parts with Cuticura ointment on the end of the finger, but do not rub; wash off the Cuticura ointment in five minutes with Cuticura soap and hot water and continue bathing for some minutes. This treatment is best on rising and retiring. At other times use Cuticura soap freely for the toilet and bath to assist in preventing inflammation, irritation and clogging of the pores, the common cause of these dis- tressing facial eruptions and other unwholesome con- ditions of the complexion and skin. Cuticura soap and ointment are sold by chemists everywhere, but a liberal sample of each, with 32-page book on the care of the complexion, hands and hair, may be had,, post- free, on application to the nearest depot: Newbery, 27, Cbarterhouse Sq., London ;R, Towns & Co., Sydney, N. S. W.; Lennon, Ltd., Cape Town; Muller, Maclean & Co., Calcutta and Bombay; " Cuticura," Boston, U. S. A. Tender-faced men should shave with Cuticura Soap Shaving Stick.

THE ROMANCE OF THE BRIDGEWATER CANAL. LECTURE TO THE -WARRINGTON SOCIETY.

BY MR. A. MOUNFIELD.

111.

Meanwhile what of the Duke?

While Brmdley wrestled with rock and soil, the Duke was facing the more exacting problem of finance. It must be remembered that the venture was a private one, and the entire cost came from a single purse. Whether by miscalculation we do not know, for the Duke from reason of defective educa- tion left little in writing, but the canal was not half done when its pioneer found himself sorely pressed for money.

===== 0002601_19120224_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROBLEM OF FINANCE.

Small wonder that his resources came to an end; for the richest private estate in the country could scarcely have endured the strain.

But a persistency which had carried a man so far as this, was not likely to easily give way. The Duke braced himself to see the thing through. He had fine houses, and promptly closed them. Servants were paid off, and horses sold. A sworn bachelor, he could choose his own manner of life, and, save that he retained his snuff box and pipe, banished every luxury,

===== 0002601_19120224_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TWIN HEROES.

He was then a man of thirty, and Brindley was twenty years older. Flung upon the horns of this dilemma, and flung together in an unequal struggle with nature and the Fates, the two men stood together like twin generals in a great campaign. Brindley buoyed up the Duke by declaring that the work could be done and every difficulty vanquished if only money could be found

Here gentle summits lift their airy brow ; Down the green slope here winds the labouring plow ; Here bathed by frequent showers cool vales are seen, Cloath'd with fresh verdure, and eternal green; Here smooth canals, across the extended plain, Stretch their long arms, to join the distant main : The sons of toil with many a weary stroke Scoop the hard bosom of the solid rock ; Resistless through the stiff opposing clay With steady patience work their gradual way, Compel the genius of th' unwilling flood Thro' the brown horrors of the aged wood ; Cross the lone waste the silver urn they pour, And cheer the barren health or sullen moor ; The traveller with pleasing wonder sees The white sail gleaming thro' the dusky trees; And views the altered landscape with surprise, And doubts the mak scenes which round him rise. Now, like a flock of BMWs, above his head Their woven wings the flying vessels spread ; Now meeting streams in artful mayes glide, While each unmingled pours a separate tide ; Now through the hidden veins of earth they flow, And visit sulphurous mines and caves below-, The ductile streams obey the guiding band, And social plenty circles round the land.

and cheered by his hopefulness the Duke strained every resource to find it. At first he sent round his agent upon a borrowing tour, and choosing first those who were least likely to refuse, borrowed from all his tenants. Week by week the estate was scoured until the tenants had paid their rents far in advance.

Rumour spread that he was insolvent, and his credit was shattered.

FRIDAY NIGHT CONFERENCES.

Many and anxious were the consultatinns held on Friday evenings in preparation for the coming pay day. Here is a picture drawn from life by Mr. Bradshaw, one of the Duke's agents. The scene is a tiny public-house upon Carrington Moss upon a Friday night. 'The party was unusually dull and silent. The Duke's ready money was exhausted and his credit at the lowest ebb. There they sat in the small parlour smoking their pipes with a pitcher of ale before them melancholy and silent. At last the Duke broke the silence by asking in a querulous tone 'Well Brindley what's to be done now? How are we to get the money? Brindley after a few long puffs answered through the smoke, Well Duke, I can't tell; I only know that if the money can be got, I can finish the Canal and that it will pay well.' 'Aye, rejoined the Duke, but where are We to get the money?' Brindley could give no help, but after a moody silence started up to say, -" Don't mind Duke; don't be cast down; we are sure to succeed after all." And with these cheery words the Duke went home to Worsley to ponder still further the problem of to-morrow's wages. The writer of this little narrative was a trusted servant of the Duke, yet even he had no idea of the straits into which his brave spirit had been pressed. He was badly in debt, and his credit so shaken that though he had great estates behind him a bill for £5OO had been refused in both Liverpool and Manchester.

DUKE AND RECTOR. Even his tithes were unpaid. And thereby hangs a tale; which I trust I shall be forgiven for telling. The Rector has sent his collector in vain, and in anger and despair went himself. As the Rector gained admission at the front door, the Duke fled from the back. But the ecclesiastic was out for prey, and was not to be baulked. Hedges and ditches proved nothing to a slim young duke of thirty. Nor were they to a parson in pursuit of his tithe. Losing breath the Duke fled into hiding in a saw pit, where the rector ran him to earth and extracted, not the cash, but a good sound promise. This, ladies and gentlemen is part of the romance of the Bridgwater Canal. THAT PEAR TREE. Much money was absorbed in the purchase of the land through which the canal }vas to run, and here the Duke had the added mental strain of dealing with claimants. Local committees seem to have been chosen to value the various lands and properties, but no one will suppose that the process was free from mental travail and delay. At Lymm the canal cut away a slice from a back garden on which a solitary pear tree grew. The owner extracted 30 guineas. I suppose modern railway promoters would consider a 'freehold pear tree cheap at 30 guineas, but to the Duke the claim was a life-long griev-

knew that they knew best, and they could tell each other so in very vigorous English. It became therefore very often a contest of enlightened stupidity, and it is said that in these contests Brindley always came off best. The old timbered rooms at Worsley Hall echoed till late at night with raised and angry voices and the men parted daggers-drawn. But in the morning it was the Duke who said: "Well, Brindley, you may be right after all; but remember it's our look-out if you are wrong."

THE WORSLEY CANAL TERMINUS, to

===== 0002601_19120224_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GRAND TRUNK CANAL.

But Brindley was seldom wrong and he lived to justify his genius over and over again. Take for example the great plan that seized him when he had planned the canal to Runcorn. Ile insisted that a great canal should be cut to join the Mersey and Severn, running through the salt districts and joining up the potteries and Birmingham. It seemed the dream of a madman; but Brindley lived to complete it, and when he died at the early age of 56 he had placed a full 360 miles of still water upon the English landscape. It is to Brindley, too, that we owe the inception of that other great and useful undertaking known to us as the Leeds and Liverpool canal.

===== 0002601_19120224_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
A STUDY FOR THE PSYCHOLOGIST

Brindley and his methods were standing puzzle to his generation. When there was a problem to be solved he went to bed and lay there a day or two until the solution came. It was a strange process for an active bustling man; but Brindley was probably wiser than he knew. He had no previous experience to go upon : he depended upon his genius, and his quiet hours in bed would be a falling back upon that sub-consciousness which physchologists would agree to tell us was his surest light and safest guide. He needed no plans, for he saw visions. It was generally too much trouble to explain schemes to other people, so he took tools and hacked them out himself. When obliged to explain things to Parliamentary Committees or other people ot lower than average intelligence he took to desperate expedients —such as chalk and cheese. But he rarely or never made paper drawings. A piece of chalk and a spacious barn door were more than enough. Brindley had no hobby except canals and he did no reading. It was only when 50 that he found time to marry; but after six years of married life he took a chill whilst travelling and succumbed, to fever. He passed away full of large projects, which if realised would have covered England with a perfect network of canals. It was fitting that he should die with canal terms upon his lips.

Some canal makers had ridden to his home at Turnhurst not knowing of his serious condition. They could not make the clay bed hold water. The dying man could scarcely gasp but said, "Puddle it." They explained that they had already puddled the clay. "Then puddle it again,—puddle it again." And Brindley's dealings in waterways were over.

===== 0002601_19120224_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
RELIEF AND TRIUMPH.

The financial crisis came in the middle of 1765. Three years heavy expenditure had completely drained the Duke's resources. He had resolved, in justice to his successors, not, to mortgage his estates; but the Worsley Canal had become a remunerative property, and no one could complain if he should mort- gage that. The summer day came when he saddled his horse, (he had retained just one when he sold his stock),and rode to the bank of Messrs. Child and Co. in London. A hasty deed of mortgage was drafted, and ,£3,800 came back in his saddle bags. In all £25,000 I was borrowed. Two more years of toiling and borrowing, and the 24 miles were cut. Under the patient and magical touch of Brindley every difficulty was met; the valleys were exalted,the crooked places made straight, and the silver streak now glistened I from Manchester to Runcorn. Thirty-four miles of navigable water of one level and without lock or hindrance—a marvel surely when considered as the work of a man who could not read his own letters nor be sure of , the spelling of his own name! But six years more of planning awaited the engineer. The flight of ten locks by which the canal was to be lowered to the Mersey had yet to be built, and it was not until the last day of 1773 that the little vessel "Heart of Oak" of 50 tons register made the first passage from the Mersey to the canal. Six hundred workmen gathered on December 31st, to roast an ox and to drink the Duke's health in every available liquor. It might be convenient here to summarise, and take leave of our pair of heroes in the hour of their triumph. But I find them con- genial company, and hope my patient hearers may enjoy yet a few more facts and a few more stories. THE TUGS OF WAR.

NEW HEADMASTERSHIP.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE AGAIN DECLINES TO RESTRICT COMPETITION.

The Warrington Education Committee again bad under discussion on Monday evening the suggested restriction of competition for the headmastership of Evelyn-street schools to Warrington teschers. It having been reported to the School Management Com- mittee that the Education Committee at their last meeting had not approved the resolution of this Com- mittee with reference to the appointment of a headmaster of the boys' department of the Evelyn-street ouncil school, it was, after some discussion, resolved that applications be invited from male certificated teachers who have served or are now serving in schools within the County Borough of IVarrington for the post of Headmaster of the Boys' department of the Evelyn- street Council school, the commencing salary for the position to he at the rate of £250 per annum up the 31st March, 1013, and to be increased or reduced after that date, in accordance with the Committee's scale of salaries, upon the average attendance of the department during that period ; that all applicants be warned that canvassing members of the Education Committee, either directly or through friends or third persons, will be considered a disqualification; and that the appointment be made by this Committee. Canon MORLEY STEVENSON, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said the committee were very much divided on the question. Some of them wished to throw the post entirely open to the whole country, while others wished to confine it to teachers in War- rington, or to those who had previously taught in War- rington. The latter coursa was carried by a very narrow majority. It was proposed to broaden the basis some- what in comparison with what had been settled before and not to be tied only to teachers who were teaching in Warrington at the present time but to open the competition to those who had previously taught in the town.

Councillor BELLIAN seconded. Mr. H. KINGDON moved an amendment that the minute be referred back. He said that during the discussion on the rstimates of the Education Com- mittee they had been talking about efficiency and making complaints that they did not get value for money. BUTrERMARKET-STREET WIDENING.

Arbitration Award.

The award of the Umpire in the recent arbitration proceedings with reference to the purshase of property required for the widening of Buttermarket-street, has been given, and is in favour of the Corporation. The executors of the late Mr. John (Intik claimed 5.:1,993 15s. from the Corporation for the purchase of their interest in shops Nos. 36, 38 and 10, Butter- market-street. At the hearing considerable evidence was called, and several barristers were engaged, the proceedings occupying two days. The award of the umpire was taken up by the Corporation on Tuesday, and we are informed that the amount is .1.:1,310 and that as this is less thin the amount offered by the Corporation the claimants will have to bear their owu costs.
