THE MYSTERIOUS PROCESSES BY WHICH HUMAN LIFE IS MAINTAINED.

An interesting article based on the most recent Scientific Research.

We know tint a huge dose of pnison may be swallowed by a hedgehog, when it is off for sleep for the winter. Sleep it will; but when the spring comes it wakes only to die. The dose of poison did no harm in its stomach, lying there as if it had been, so to say, in the hedgehogs waistcoat pccket. But when the suspended activities were renewed, and the blood began to absorb the poison from the stomach in the sprit,g, the poison really entered tke living hedgehog and killed it.

In a word, we live—or die, as the case may be—not by what we swallow, but by what we digest and absorb—not by what fills us, but by what feeds us We may eat to our heart's content, or, as is more likely, to our heart's extreme discomfort; but unless we absorb the ncurishment in what we eat, it does no good.

a 0

MAN AND THE MUG. A vrrAL DIFFERENCE.

You cannot fill the body with food as you fill a mug with milk. The mug merely holds the milk, but does not absorb it. The milk never becomes part of the china which encloses it. But your food, if it is to nourish you, must become part of your body. You are different from the mug because yru are alive. The mug could be fllltd, just as a chair is vainished or a cloth dyed, iemaining passive all the while. But your body is alive. It is made up of living ee1:8, which behave just as men and women do when food is set before them. _ .

They choose and refuse, taste and reject, or taste and gobble. The mug could be filled at any time; but the cells—being alive—cannot be fed except when they are willing to eat. Unless tbeee cells do their work, the food is not absorbed —just as, vvnen you visit a prison, you may walk through the corridors and leave the building:again, withcut being held back in the cells.

* * *

THE STOMACH IS A COUIIN AND AN OVEN-

===== 0002601_19120224_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT A SIEVE.

Food is not absorbed in the stomach. The stomach is a receptacle, a churn, and an oven. It is not a sieve. It holds and mixes and cooks what you have eaten. But it cannot sift the nourishing part of food into your flesh and blood, nor finally reject the waste. When its work is done, it merely passes the food on to the bowel, where the vital act of absorption takes place.

Diagram showing the Vali (A. A

Here the surface is studded with countless projee tions, called vil/i, covered with minute cells.l hese cell- take the food that passes over them, selecting just what suite their purpose. They break down ea or wheat proteins into little pieces, and then build the pieces up differently, so as to make human proteins— the special proteins that are found in the blood of man and nowhere else in the world. Having con- structed these human proteins out of the ford so scented, the cells pass their products into the thin- walled blood-vessels. The proteins are carried by the blood throughout the whole body,and are deposited and employed where they are mcst needed to build up flesh and muscle. It is thus apparent that a person is nournished not by what he eats, but by the nourish- ment which is absorbed by the cells of the vi//i. If these do not do their work properly,no amount of food can nourish the body.

* * a

SETTING THE VILLE TO WORK.

The question of how to assist the cells to convert into human proteins mcre of the food passing over them has long been a problem of science, but the recent series of remarkable feeding experiments carried out on human beings have at last thrown a new light on the subject. They lave shown that Bovril will do this. For they have proved that the body absorbs extra nourishment to the extent of from 10-20 times the amount of Bovril added to other food.

- As has already been explained, the body normally absorbs a part only of the nourishment in the food taken. Bovril, however, not only is a food in itself, but increases the amount of other food absorbed. The experiments have proved that Bovril contains en attive Trinciple— the operation of which opens out to scientists a new and fascinating field of study. By irte of this principle Bovril so act upon the cells of the rilli that they ere enabled to perform with greater thorough- ness their work of extracting nourishment from what we eat.

W ERRAL AND BIRKENHEAD AGRICULTURAL

SoCIETY.

The Council of the Wirral and Birkenhead Agricul- tural Society Lave appointed Mr H C Ardern, secretary to the Leicester Agricultural Society, to succeed Mr Arthur H Edwardson as secretary. It is expected that he will take up his new appointment in about two months time. The fact that Mr. Edward Eon severs his connection with tbe Society will be received with regret by sub- scribers and supporters who year after year have visited the show. His association with the Society dates back 25 years, and his services to the Council have been of the most loyal character. Mr. Edwardson's advice proved roost valuable in the dark days through which the Society passed, and more especially after the calamity that befell the show during the storm in the Bidston Hill days. Mr. Edwardson was instrumental, with others, in securing the present tine site for the annual show at Bebington, and leaves the Scciety when prosperity is being experienced. Mr. Edwardson is known all over the country as a most capable secretary. He has witnessed the Wirral and Birkenhead Shows increase from what might be termed a parochial class of exhibit to a show of first-class importance. The Council of the Society decided in view of Mr. Edwardson's long and faithful services to make him an honorary life member, an honour which will be appreciated by the recipient and his friends. Although Mr. Edwardson's resignation takes effect on the 23rd inst., he, at the request of the Council, has agreed to remain in office until bis tuccrssor enters upon his new duties.

CHAPTER Ix. A WOMAN'S LAUGH.

To a man of Clifford Hawke's temperament the idea of being constantly under the eyes of an enemy. albeit that enemy's manners left nothing to be desired—had the effect of deep- ening his hate and fear of him. Paul Stein- way was in his office, behaving like any of the other clerks there—in fact, his quick grasp of financial prospectuses and deals made even Clifford Hawke marvel and curse inwardly. He had hoped that City life and the irlaiomc office hours would soon tire the bushman's energetic outdoor spirit, but on the contrary Paul Steinway seemed to thrive in spite of the fact that the sunburn left his face and he grew pale like his fellow Londoners. The two men never spoke except on business, and no one in the big offices of Messrs. Traine, Hawke and Co. guessed that a secret hung between them, linking them in a bond of sus- picious hate. One day something happened that made Hawke realise that Paul Steinway was as ambitious a man as he was, and showed that he did not mean to sink his dominant spirit in an office stool all hie; life. "May I speak to you for a few moments? " Steinway said on one occasion when he chanced to be alone with Hawke in his private den at the office. "Is it important? " he replied curtly. "I am very busy now." Steinway smiled. "To me it is very important, probably you might consider it the same—later on." "Well, what is it? Are you tired of this sort of work, or do you want more money?" Hawke said slowly. 'I thought we had dis- cussed that subject before." "I am ;ite satisfied with the present state of affairs," Steinway replied, "only I want you to give me a letter of introduction to Mr. Lestocq, of the firm of Lestocq and Water- field, the diamond brokers. I heard you talking to Lestocq yesterday," he went on, "and he mentioned something about looking out for a good partner." Hawke broke into a sneeeing laugh. "And you, a penniless Crlonial, think you would be a fitting partner for 'King' If2stoeq, do you? " Steinway flushed. "I do," he replied. "You see I have not been idle the few weeks I heave been in your office, and a little private speculation has made a fairly rich man of ine. 1 am penni- less no longer—though still a. Colonial.' OUR SERIAL STORY.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]

A BID FOR A BRIDE

BLANCHE EARDLEY, Author of " Kitty Bell—Actress," "The Lady Killer," &c. SYNOPSIS.

CLlrronn ITAWTE, financier and secretary of the Amalga- mated Copper Company, is about to set the seal on his ambition. At the age of forty he has the consent of the managing director of the saine company to propose mar- riage to his only daughter, Stella Trait" a beautiful girl. Though scarcely sure of herself she accepts him. Hawke returns to his chambers, and receives at letter left during Ids absence. Though it comes from a former friend, Paul Steinway, Hawke is by no means pleased; evidently this man knows too much of the financiers former days. At the door of a house to which Bartle has driven Stella Trains in his motor, a girl in the crowd faints. Stella asks hawks to lift her up, but instead he sends for a servant. A young artist guest comes to the fainting woman's rescue and carries her into the house. Later Stella and the artist—Jaspar Tennant—discuss what shall be done for the poor woman—Lotty Bond—and this leads to an invita- tion for Stella to visit the artist's studio. In a conversation with his hostess—Lady Mary Stainer—Tennant refers to the subject which is his principal aim in life—the avenging of his sister on the man who deserted her and their child. _ _ _ . .

....... _..._.. Isebei Frant, •• poorrelatieu" of Stella Trainos, who is in love with Clifford Hawke, is sitting, in the nark. Tier attention is directed to a man whose eyes gleam with hate, and who is evidently Intently watching for some person. As he walks past her an unposted letter falls from his poCket, which Isobel picks up with the intention of re- turning. The address of Clifford Hawke upon it Causes her to change her plans, and once in her room she reads the letter. It contains the secret of Clifford Hawke's past In fulfilment of her promise, Stella visits ,Taspar I mutant's ',Oldie', in company with Lady Mary Stairer. They are joined by Hawke and Isobel, the former in a vile temper because he has learnt on the way that the girl is in posses- Sioll of his secret. A picture of which Lotty Bond is tile model also draws Tennant into disclosures which make Ilawke tremble and wonder Imw much he suspects or knows. While the financier Is contemplating how best to crush them two, a third antagonist awaits him at his house—Pant Steinway. . .

MosAn offers Steinway £3OOO for his silence, but nothing save a post under the man lie is blackmailing will satisfy Paul. With Lotty Bond MI their protege Stella and Jaspar are frequently brought together, and, at a subsequent visit to thestudie, Tennant is unaole to restrain from an avowal of the love he hears for Stella, for he has guessed that she does not love Ilawke. In the midst of the avowal Hawke himself appears—he has overheard. Cynically indignant, Ilawke takes Stella off without A word to Tennant. CHAPTER VIII. "THERE IS NOTHING TO FORGIVE."

The approaching wedding of Stella Traino to Clifford Hawke drew nearer and nearer, until there remained only three weeks to the wedding-day. Since that scene in Jaspar Tennant's studio when he had let go the floodgates of his secret before her, she k not seen him, nor had she mentioned his name once to any of the people who could have given her information about him. Only once did she hear anything, and that was when she and Isobel were leaving a Bond- street dressmaker's and they met Lady Mary Stainer, who took them back to tea with her. Before they left she said abruptly, "I sup- pose you know that Jaspar Tennant has left town?"

Stella had felt the colour mount to her brow,

"I didn't know," she had answered, and then had changed the subject round to her trousseau, and the thin ice had been skil- fully evaded. But since that incident she had felt even less interest than before in all the many details that usually delight the heart of a prospective bride. She had ex- pected Clifford Hawke to mention the scene he had interrupted, but beyond a certain bull-dog-like watchfulness he had ignored it, and she was grateful to him for his toler- - alone

ance.

na I6obel

One day she ana —_ were lunching a. at the Savoy. They had been expecting Mr. Traine and Clifford Hawke to join them, but a telephone message had been brought to Stella that her fattier and Mr. Hawke would be prevented from joining them. She had felt glad, but Isobel's dark face had flushed with disappointment. "Don't you think, Stella," she began pre- sently, "that Mr. Hawke has been looking very seedy lately? " "I haven't noticed it," she replied care- lessly; "why?" "Oh, I can hardly answer that," Isobel laughed, "but several people have noticed it. He looks like a man who has something on his mind—a secret, to be quite frank. I thought you might know it." "A secret! " Stella repeated. "My dear Isobel, so far as I know Clifford might have a thousand; but I would not dream of question- ing him, even if I thought about it," she added. "I don't care for prying into people's inner lives." Isobel looked at her curiously. She hated this beautiful cousin of hers, who possessed all that she herself coveted. Wealth, posi- tion, and the man—the one man she wanted for herself above all others! She had never eared for her cousin even in the days when they had been schoolgirls together, and had shared the same bedroom. She had been jealous of her then, when, at the end of a term, Stella would receive a couple of prizes, and she would go home empty handed. And now the petty jealousy of the schoolgirl had developed into the dangerous insincerity of a venomous woman. "I can understand that attitude towards a man one doesn't care about," she said slowly, "but if I loved a man I would want to reign in the innermost sanctum of his heart; to share every secret, whether good or evil; to be all in all to him, so that no other woman could exist as far as he was concerned—and no other man so far as I was."

"That love is very rare I should think," Stella replied, but as she spoke there flashed before her a forbid4en memory of someone who might perhaps have inspired her with such a love—who had such a love for her—and she flushed suddenly. Isobel saw the blush, and smiled. She knew quite well that the love was on Clifford Hawke 's side only, that Stella either had her heart in her own keeping, or had given it to someone else.

"Mr. Hawke's love for you is that—" she belan, then stopped suddenly. Two men had taken a table close to theirs, and had just ordered lunch, and as she looked at the one facing her, Isobel knew him at once as the man of the lost letter—the man called Paul Steinway. He had evidently prospered since that day in the Park. The lean hungry look had left his face, and his cheeks were no longer hollow, and he wore the ordinary dress of the type of men who fre- quented smart restaurants. Then she glanced at his companion. He was older and more of a rough diamond than the other, and had that unmistakable stamp of the life-long Colonial about him. She won- dered what they were talking about. Their conversation seemed engrossing, and she felt thrit elie would like to be somewhere where she could hear without being seen. As they rose from the table, she followed Stella, and when they reached the lounge she said suddenly : "How stupid °Cm! I must have dropped my handkerchief. Wait while I •go and get it."

"Send a waiter," Stella said. "I'd rather go myself," she replied hastily. "I won't be a second." She reached the table by the window where they had been sitting and picked up the hand- kerchief she had dropped on purpose. As she did so she glanced again at the two men at the next table. The older one was saying in his loud bluff voice, Well, tell me now, old man, where you are staying, and I'll come and call. "

With a splendid assumption of careleasnces Isobel opened her jewelled bag and tucked her handkerchief into it. The voice of the younger man replied laughingly: "At present I am staying at 98, Claverton- street, but now I'm in regular employment I shall soon be 'changing my address." Isobel moved away. She was satisfied Tore than she had anticipated being. She had gone back to have one more look at the man who had been the innocent cause of her learning Clifford Hawke's secret; now ^he. had his add::,..ss as well,. _

•

in the lan!:aniette tnat was taring ner ana Stella back to Park-lane she was very silent. Her energetic mind was already plotting and planning, anti the presence of her cousin kept her from giving herself up to the new idea that had occurred to her. Stella was the first to break the silence. "Isobel, do you mina going on home alone in the car? I want to get out at Hyde Park. corner."

"Why not drive first where you want to go and then I can go on afterwards?" Isobel re- plied. Stella smiled.

"I don't think that will do. I am going to a locality where the car would attract too much attention; you can use it if you want to go anywhere." "Am I permitted to know where you are going?" Isobel said lightly. She was in-. wardly burning with curiosity as to what her cousin could possibly want to do that she conld not take the car with her.

"Only to pay a call," Stella said gently. A few minutes later they had separated, and Isobel smiled ernicallv as she saw her cousin hail a hansom and disappear in the traffic.

"Is it a mission of charity or a rendez- vous?" she muttered. "It seems to me that my dear cousin is beginning to have secrets of her own."

In the meantime Stella was leaning back in the hansom that was bearing her towards Westminster. She was glad to have escaped from Isobel's prying eyes and unsympa- thetic presence, and to let the mask of gaiety fall from her face. She was so tired of every- thing. Tired or the part she was playing— the days that were so dull and empty in spite of the weddiitg preparations, and most of all she was tired of the role that she would have to go through for the rest of her life. She was longing for freedom—the life of little Lotty Bond would be preferable to the gilded cage against the bars of which 4e was vainly battering her wings. At leakt Lotty did see and talk to that one man whom nte had let her meet too late! How she would love to change places with the artist's model! She, the daughter of a millionaire, and the future wife of a man who was on the high road to be one! The hansom pulled up outside a big build- ing that seemed to be swarming with child- ren, and the cabman informed her that he could not drive into the courtyard, so she paid the fare he demanded and got out. It was with great 'difficulty she found the part of the tenement where Lotty lived. The children in the court gazed at her with open expressions of wonder and admiration; her cream cloth gown and black picture hat had never before set fire to such fuel of approval as that which it ignited in the youthful breasts of the inhabitants of Vincent Build- ings. At last she reached No. 14, where Lotty lived, and knocked at the door. After a few moments' pause it was opened by a woman with a face so sodden with drink that the in- nate good humour of it shone like a dim star obscured by fog. "I want to see Lotty Bond," Stella said. "She lives here, doesn't she?"

The woman nodded. "She does, lidy; she's met with a accident."

"An accident! Oh, poor girl! Take me to her,"

The next moment she was in a tiny ill- ventilated room, very poorly furnished, and on a bed lay Lottie Bond, her fair face flushed with fever, and her blue eyes glitter- ing with excitement. Stella went up to her quickly. "Oh, ray dear child! How did this happen? I am so sorry. When you called on me that Tuesday you were quite well." Lotty smiled faintly. "I know, Miss Traine, but it was coming away from you that I was knecked down by a motor and hurt. But I'm getting better now," she added bravely. "You poor girl!" Stella said gently, bend- ing over her and kissing the white flower- like face, "You must get well, and then come arid stay with the. Would you care for that?"

The girl's face flamed. "Care !" she said, with a choking little cry. "Why, it would just be heaven! Mr. Ten- nant came to see me yesterday, and he was saying that I should go into the country.' "I thought he was away from London," Stella said quickly. "Oh, no, miss. He talked of it, but hain't gone yet. He comes to see me very often," she added naively. Stella was silent. She felt a great- throb- bing in her veins, a joyous uplifting of. her heart that she knew was wrong, yet which she could not control, try how she might. He was in London after all, and sometimes came to see Lotty. Oh, lucky Lotty ! "And is he still going on with the pie- tore?" she asked.

Lotty shook her head. "NO, he seems to hare lost all interest in it. He said that when I was all right he'd go abroad." Then he meant to go away after all! He was staying just to see Lotty was, left in good hands. "When do you think you will be able to be moved, dear?" she said quietly. "I suppose the owner of the motor car that knocked you down is paying your expenses." "No miss," Lottie answered. "He never even ;topped until the policeman made him. But it's Mr. Tennant who is doing evezything for me, and is paying Mrs. Murphy to come and nurse me." Stella's face flamed with indignation. "It's not right that the owner of the car should escape his responsibilities like this. Mr. Ten- nant should find out the name of the man and make him pay." As she spoke a shadow darkened the door- way, and Jaspar Tennant stood facing her in silent amazement.

For a moment they gazed at eath other, noting every detail and shade in each other's appearance; then Stella found her voice and spoke. "How do you do, Mr. Tennant? I did not expect to see you here. I came to see Lotty, and have just heard of her accident." "Yes," he answered, "it was a narrow escape for poor Lotty,• Miss Traine •, I sup- pose Mr. Hawke told you about the acci- dent?" "Mr. Hawke !" she said wonderingly. "How should be? I don't understand." Then an exclamation broke from her as she read con- fusion and dismay in his face. "You mean it was his car that knocked Lottie down?" she said it in trembling voice ; "his car, and —and he never troubled to call and see what he could do !"

"I don't think Mr. Hawke quite realised what had happened." Tennant said quickly. He could not bear to see that shamed look on her face. Much as he hated the man she was going to marry he would not, for her sake, show him to be the callous blackguard he was.. "The whole thing was so sudden, and he seemed in a great hurry, I believe," he went on, conscious he was making a very lame excuse in favour of the absent man. Stella raised her head haughtily. She had gone .strang,ely.

"Please don't let us discuss it," she said quietly. "What you say may be correct, of course." Then she turned to Lottie. "I will come again, dear, and when you are better you must come and stay with me. Per- haps you would like to do some little light things for me. Do you sew?" The girl's face lit up eagerly. "Oh, yea, mother taught me to do lace work and all kinds of embroidery. I should love to come to you, Miss Traine." Stella bent and kissed her. "You shall, dear. Good-bye." Then she turned to Tennant. "You will let me know when she is better, Mr. Ten- nant?" "Yes" he said gravely. "I will write you, Miss Traine."

When she left the room he followed her. "You must allow me to put you in a hansom," he said quickly. "This is a rough neighbour- hood. You ought not to have come alone." "I am not afraid," she replied„ and then there fell upon them a sudden silence. Ten- nant broke it abruptly. "Miss Traine. I owe you an anolociv for—

Whitley.

THE LATE MRS CLIFFE.—The funeral took place in the churchyard on Sunday of the late Mrs Thomas Cliffe, an old resident in Higher Whitley. The Vicar conducted the cervice, which was attended by members of the family and sympathizing friends. Many beauti- ful wreaths were sent. Orford.

BIBLE CLASS SOCIAL.—A ,very successful social organized by the Bible classes of Ortord Church, was held in the school on Thursday night, the Lith inst. Games, competitions, and dancing were indulged in until mid-night. The ladies' hopping competition was won by Miss M Evans, 7; minutes, and the gent's by Mr B Wilson, 11 minutes. Miss M Sloane was the pianist for dancing, and the M C's were Messrs J Swinton and W H Houghton.

INDEPENDENT METHODIST CHURCH, FEARNHEAD.— A grand concert was held in the schoolroom in connec- tion with the above place of worship. Mr C H Harri- son occupied the chair, and various songs and recita- tions were given during the evening. Miss C Barton in her song" I'm trying so hard to forget you" was well received. The children's action drill was very well performed and earned great applause. Mr E Barton caused much merriment with his recitation' The quack doctor." The dialogues " Four after one," " Mrs Muddle," and "Paddy's mistake," were one and all well acted and caused much laughter. Miss E A Bate greatly amused her audience in reciting " An inventor's wife." The proceeds go towards the cost of a piano.

Penketh.

PENIIETII WESLEY GUILD.—In connection with the above a lantern lecture was given in the Wesleyan School on Wednesday evening under the auspices of the literary section of the Guild. One of the jaint secre- taries, Mr E A Murphy, was the lecturer his subject being "A tour through Switzerland." Mr Murphy having spent his holidays in Switzerlandklast summer, was able to give some very interesting information on the different slides which were put on the screen. Aithough such an educative evening deserved a far larger compauyi than was present, those who had availed themselves of the appor,unities were highly pleased. Mrs Dodds. who presided, and Mrs Garnett voiced the feeling of the meriting to Mr Murphy for his able and interesting lecture. Mr J Woodward manipulated the lantern.

Moore.

WESLEY GUILD.—On Wednesday evening Alderman Bennett, of Padgate, gave a very interesting lecture on" Some Irish Reminiscences." After visiting Ireland some 20 years ago he wrote a book on the subject. Mr. Bennett said he did not propose to deal with the lecture in a political sense. It was the third holiday the lecturer had spent in the " Emerald Isle " and the chief plsces be visited were Dublin, Wicklow, Limerick, and Kilrush. Whilst at Wicklow Mr. Bennet t stayed at St Joseph's Monastery. He gave a very interesting descrip- tion of the Monastery and its beautiful surroundings, and also the kindly wty in which he was received and treated. From Wicklow he Bailed down the River Shannon to Limerick, where again he was struck with the hospitality of the people. From Limerick he sailed still further down the river (which he said no words of his could &scribe) to Eilrush. Miss Dutton proposed a hesrty vote of thanks to Mr. Bonnet which was seconded by Mr Meldrum. The chair was taken by Mr John Jackson.

Hill CHM. CONCERT AT THE BAPTIST SCHOOL.—A very success- ful concert, which had bsen arranged by Mrs. Kenworthy was given on Thursday evening at the Baptist school- room at Hill Cliff°, in aid of a fund for a new organ for the Chapel. The programme, which was an excel- lent one, was commenced by Miss Parkinson with a pianoforte solo, played with great precision and taste. This was followed by a song The valley of the sea," front Mr. Ratcliffe, very pleasingly given in a voice of rare quality and flexibility. After that a recital by Miss Corney by way of variation, entitled "At a society wedding," was so highly appreciated as to call forth an encore, for which Miss Conley gave a recitation in verse called "The usual way" followed by a further recitation by Mr. Chedburn illustrative of some of the life on the road, which was also highly appreciated and called forth another encore, for which Mr. Chednurn gave " When I was a boy at school." Then the "Jewel song" from Gounod's Faust was beautifully rendered by Mrs. Booth, and an encore demanded and acceded to by an equally beautiful rendering of " Snow flakes." Another pleasing variation in the programme was in the form of a violin solo, in the playing of which Mrs. Haviour evinced such command ot her instrument and perfect taste as pro- duced another call for an encore. Mrs Havi our graciously responded to this, and was followed by a most tasteful rendering of "0 dry those tears " by Mrs Kenworthy ; the beauty of this song was enhance d by a violin obligato. Here also an encore was asked for and given in a pleasing rendition of " Idle words. After this an lot erval of a few minutes was declared for refreshments. The second half of the programme was (qually pleasing and again led off by a most tasteful rendering of a selection from Mendelssohn's music without words by Miss Parkinson. A variation was given in the form of a duet by Mesdames Booth and Kenworthy entitled "Over the hawthorn hedge." A very determined attempt was made by the audience to elicit an encore, but as the evening was getting far advanced the lady artistes bad to decline. Miss Corny and Mr. Cbedburn gave the dialogue entitled" If I were you "and a very successful programme was brought to a close by the singing of the doxology.

Stockton Heath,

Nunsitsua ASSOCIATION.—Mr. J. Longland presided at the annual meeting of the Nursing Association which was held in the Church Schools on Monday evening. There were also present the Rev J Collins, Mr W H Woodcock, Mr and Mrs T Baxter, Mrs Milling, Mr E Bradshaw, Mr F Timperley, Mr Bouchier, Mr Grocott, Miss Willett and Mrs Silcock. The fifth annual report of the committee, presented by Mr E Bradshaw stated that the total number of cases visited during 1911 was 161, viz : Medical oases 83, and surgical%cases 81. The total number of visits paid was 3,214. Of the patient 4 131 were convalescent or cured, 20 had died, seven bad been transferred to hospital and three remained on the books. The committee thanked Miss Bainbridge, Mesdames Bradshaw, Crane, Hewitt, T J Lee, Milling, Richardson, H J Westbrook and the Rev J Collins for old linen, the Rev J Collins for the use of the school for committe meetings, all those who had so kindly collected for the nourishment fund, and Nurse Wherritt for her efficient services. Three recommendations had been issued during the year to convalescent homes and one toManchester Sanatorium.—The report was adopted Mr. Bouchier, honorary treasurer, in presenting his report, said at the end of the fifth year of the associa- tion there was a balance in the bank on general account of £6l 16s sd. The subscriptions for the year t 4, g9l 181 2d, Mr Vera 13 Davios was re-elected ;president and the vice-presidents are Lady Greenall, tho Rev J Collins, Mrs Down, Mr .1 C Parr, Mr Henry Greenall, Mr L Davies, Mr W Richardson and Mr A Down. Mr J W Bouchier was again elected bon. treasurer, and the hon. secretaries, Mrs Milling and Mr E Bradshaw, were re-appointed. The Committee of Management and the Executive Committee were also re-appointed. Mr Woodcock proposed a vote of thanks to the Ladies' Committee, which, he said, did all the worts,. The nurse was under their direct supervision.—Mr E Bradshaw seconded and the resolution was carried unanimously. PAROCHIAL WHIST DRIVE.—A successful parochial bridge and whist drive was held in the Church Room on Tuesday evening. There was a large attendance, the function having been organized by Mrs Henry Greenall, Mrs Armitage and friends, with Mr W Mclntyre as hon secretary and Mr T J Lee as bon treasurer. The room presented a very pleasing appearance with its tasteful decorations of streamers and bunting and plants kindly lent by Mrs H Greenall and Mrs. Armitage. There were forty-two tables for whist and bridge, the M C's for the whist being Messrs W Mclntyre and F Timperley, and for bridge Messrs Graenall and Armitage. Gentlemen : 1, Mr Mclntyre ;2, Miss Cortese (playing as gentleman.) At the close Mrs Armitage distributed the prizes to the winners. It is expected the proceeds, which are to be devoted to parochial purposes, will realise about £2O.

LYMM WF.SLEY GUILD.—On Monday evening Mr. J. Upson, J.P., of Warrington, delivered a most interesting and instructive lecture on "The Origin of Words," showing what influence the different historical events had in the making of our language, and how we have progressed from mere signs to the more intelligible words.

LYMINI MAY QUEEN FESTIVAL.—In connection with the above Festival a public meeting was held in Hurst's Pavilion on Tuesday n;ght to see if it were advisable to carry on the May Queen ceremony this year. 111rJ D B Smith made a capable chairman and after a lengthy discussion it was resolved to form a sub-committee to go fully into the finances of the festival before the next public meeting. FRIENDLY SOCIETIES AND THE INSURANCE BILL.— In connection with the Lymm Division of the Order of the Sons of Temperance Friendly Society, a public meeting was held in the Primitive Methodist School- room, on Thursday night, when Mr Walter Davies, of Manchester, continued his address of a fortnight ago on" The Insurance Bill and how it effects Friendly Societies." He roughly outlined the five fundamentals of the Bill which he had previously spoken upon and dwelt chiefly on the workings and benefits of the Act, especially as it related to the Friendly Societies. After the close of his address he answered the several questions which we:e asked. A vote of thanks proposed by Mr Mather and seconded by Mr Barnes was carried with acclamation. Mr J D B Smith ably performed the duties of chairman.

LYMM CHORAL SOCIETY.-Our Continental friends aver that the musical education of we English people ends with Mendelssohn. Though not strictly accurate, we are afraid there is a modicum of truth beneath the statement, and that we have too many devotees, worshipping at the shrine of a composer who, apart from his wonderful gift of melody, is not exactly in the front rank of constructive musicians. It was Mendelssohn's misfortune, from a musical standpoint to be born with a silver spoon in his mouth, sod to pass through his too short life with the monotonous calm of a tranquil dream. Had his circumstances demanded a stubborn fight for existence, had the stern realities of life called the time whilst he penned his music,some echo of his struggles would certainly have found their way into his compositions and supplied that rugged element which is so conspicuously absent. His 95th Psalm, "Come, let us sing " presented for hearing by the Lymm Choral Society in the Parish Cdurch last Sunday afternoon,is of the usual Mendelsshon type—melodious, graceful and strictly orthodox. Speaking of the performance itself," 0 come, let us worship" and" Come let us sing" were perhaps the pick of the choral numbers, being rendered in good style and with due regard to light and shade, though the position of the singers appeared to detract from the effect of the performance. Mr J II Draper, tenor, of Warrington, who was the soloist, is possessed of a pleasing voice which he used with intelligence and good effect. Mr William Cooke was an efficient con- ductor and the society were fortunate in securing the services of Mr 0 G Smith, of Oughtrington, as organist for the occasion. A collection in aid of the Lymm and District Nursing Association was taken up and amounted to over £5.

DEATH Or Mn, W. J. WRIGIIT.—WA regret to have to announce the death of Mr William James Wright, of Ash Villa, Lymm, and Bridge-street, Manchester, who passed away with tragic suddenness on Tuesday at 10.40 a.m. Mr Wright though over seventy yerrs of age had lost little of the vigour of his earlier manhood and i arose on Tuesday morning feeling quite n his usual state of health. He was partaking of breakfast preparatory to leaving for business in Manchester, and his wife going from the room to get a cup of coffee,left him in the chair reading the morning paper. She was her rifled on returning to the room to find her husband dead. The sudden and grevio ua shock has naturally prostrated the lady and widespread sympathy is extended both to the newly bereaved widow and family. For about 20 years Mr. Wright has been connected with the Cherry-lane Baptist Chapel, officiating as one of its most earnest deacons. He was a generous though unostentatious gentleman and that he did not parade his benevolence has been clearly manifested since his decease, many people coming to the family and whilst expressing their sympathy have testified to the numerous kindnesses he has, unknown even to his family, extended to them. Within recent years he has had a branch works of his business—gold beating—erected at Lymm, where many persons have found employment. Mr. Wright took a great interest in horticulture, working bard, and subscribing liberally in furtherance of the various local flower shows. He was a Liberal in politics and as a Free Churchman had very broad views. The funeral took place yesterday afternoon, the first part of the service being held in Higher-lane Baptist Chapel. Mr Thomas Smith of Halifax, officiated, and Mr House, of London, announced the hymns " We've no abiding city here" and " Rock of ages." In the course of an address Alr.Smith said he had known the deceased for over fifty years and knew him to be a true profess- ing follower of his Master. Evidence of the respect in which the deceased gentleman was held was afforded by the large congregation—Miss Brookes accompanied the hymns on the organ.—The cortege proceeded to the Pariah churchyard, where the interment took place. The chief mourners were :—The widow, three sons, two daughters, relatives Mr Bate (co-deacon), Messrs Schofield, Croft, Barlow, Clayton, Isherwood and Broomfield (lay preachers of the denomination) and ernployees.—ln accordance with the deceased's wish, there were no floral tributes.—The funeral arrangements were in the bands of Mr W G Groves. The bearers were : Messrs Knowles, Leather, Lea and Moston. FAREWELL TO LOCAL EMIGRANTS.—OR Wednesday night in the Primitive Methodist Schoolroom a meeting was held which was as interesting as it is fortunately rare, namely, a valedictory gathering in connection with the departure of Messrs. John Wibberley, and Ernest Warham and their families, who sailed on Friday for Canada. Mr. G. L. Welford (Sunday School Superintendent) presided over the menting, and was supported by Messrs. W. T. Moss and H. Champ (Assistant Sunday School Superintendents) and J. Edwards (choirmaster). After a hymn and prayer Mr. A. B Edwards appropriately sang "Peg away," the rendering of which brought forth as an encore " Rolling down to Rio." Refresh- ments provided by the lady teachers and other helpers, were handed round, pianoforte music in the meanwhile being ably provided by Mr E A Edwards. Mr T Newton the next soloist on the programme, gave an artistic rendering of "0 song Divine." Mr Welford in his speech before the presentation stated that he had known Mr and Mrs J Wibberley practically all their lives as they had both been connected with the Sunday school and church from childhood. Mr and Mrs E Warham though comparatively recent corners, had thrown in their lot with the society and had rendered valuable service.—Mr W T Moss in a short and appropriate speech then presented to Mr J Wibberley a travelling rug. Mr. Welford also presented him with a fountain pen on behalf of the Sunday school, of which Mr Wibberley hes been secretary.— After a suitable response from the recipient, a like presentation was made to Mr E Warta= by Mr Champ, who reminded his audience that he was an amateur astronomer, and therefore looked with equanimity on the comparatively trifling distance which would shortly separate the audience from their esteemed friends.—Mr Warham acknowledged the gift, ar,cl gave some humorous but sound advice to the friends be bad been associated with so closely.—As a final contribution,

Miss M J Appleton sang " When we from one another parting" with marked effect, and after another hymn the proceedings closed with the Benediction.—Tbe meeting was impressive without being heavy, and the departing families go away with the very best wishes of the many friends whom they are leaving in the" Old Country."

WEDNESDAY.—Before Mr W P Rylands (in the chair), Major Groves, Messrs P J Ridgway, A W Callender, S W Gould, J Coxon and H Holden._ _ _

CYCLISTS .SUMMONED.—George—Hewitt, butcher, Church Green, Lymm, was summoned for having ridden a bicycle without a light at Heatley 012 the 13th January. The case was adjoured.—James Bythell, carter, Moss- lane, Warburton, for riding a bicycle on the footpath in Heatley-road, Warburton, on the 15th January, was fined 55., and for riding a bicycle without a light in Higher-lane on the 28th January,John Ward, blacksmith Wellfteld-street, Warrington, was fined ss.—For a similar offence in Warrington-road on the 27th January, James Mcflale, labourer, Northwich-road, High Leigh, was fined ss.

CASE DISMISSED.—James Ogden, bricksetter, Rush Green, Lymm, was summoned for having made use of obscene language in Eagle-brow at 11.30 p in on the 10th February. He denied the offence and after hearing evidence the magistrates dismissed the case.

TERRITORIALS IN TROUBLE.—Herbert Rigby, labourer, Hush-green, and Walter Haddon, labourer, Pepper-street, Lymm, were summoned for having made use of obscene language on Booth's-hill on the 10th February. An officer stated that the defendants, wear- ing the uniform of Territorials, were rolling in the road and making use of filthy language. They were cautioned but would not desist. They said they had been to a dinner of the Territorials. The Chairman, in imposing a fine of 2s Gd each, said that it was a very serious offence. When men joined the Territorial Army they were servants of His Majesty, and when wearing his uniform, they should treat it with proper respect and not abuse it.

CART WITHOUT REAR: Ltoirr.-,—James Higgins, farm labourer, Cherry-lane, Lymm, was fined les for having been in charge of a horse and lurry with an ordinary swing lamp on the rear of the lurry instead of a red light. He had since left the district, but at the close of the Court he put in an appearance, and the fine of 20s which had been imposed in his absence, was reduced to one of 10s.

THE CATAPULT DANGER.—Arthur Burgess, (20), labourer, Canal Side, Lymm, was charged with wantonly discharging a bullet from a catapult at 1.40 on the 14th January, to the danger of Isabel Warren.—She stated that on the day in question she was standing at the corner of the road in front of the Cross. She saw a number of boys across the road and she began walking up and down. She had hardly been there five minutes when she crossed the road and a lead bullet crashed through her hat. She looked across but could not see anything in the youth's hand. If the missile had been a stone she would not have taken any notice of it. The defendant had admitted the oftence and had said he discharged the bullet from a catapult. It had gone through her hat and stuck in the lining.—Prisoner said he was very sorry. He had found the catapult on the road from Northwich and be could not help but try it. He had no intention of hitting anyone and did not see anyone across the road.—Sergeant Washington said he saw the young men at the corner near the cross and the lady complained to him that defendant had shot a bullet which had stuck in her hat. He went down the road and found the youths and when they were going to the Police Station Burgess gave the catapult to his mother who was near the road. When he got to the station he said they could search him and they would not find a catapult. Witness had received many complaints about similar offences.—The Chairman said it was a very dangerous practice and defendant would be fined 20s.

WESLEYAN HOME MISSIONARY ANNIyRRSARY.— Sermons in connection with the above were preached in the Wesleyan Church on Sunday. In the morning the Rev H Williamson occupied the pulpit. In the evening Rev H Middlewood preached and the choir rendered the anthem "Turn Thy face from my sins." On Monday evening the annual meeting was held. Mr Duckworth presided and spoke of the great practical success attending the work of the missions conducted by Rev S F Collier in Manchester and by Rev J Jackson in Liverpool. Rev E Morgan presented the annual report of the Warrington Circuit, and commended upon the good position it occupied, having contributed during the past year the sum of £47 3s 51. The chief speaker was the Rev J H G reeves, of Rochdale. After a brief general survey of the Home Mission work of the Wesleyan Methodist Church the rev gentleman spoke more particularly of his own special work in Rochdale. Among other novel methods adopted were mentioned : Shortening the service to one hour and having half-an-hour after the meeting ; the use of a brake to go round the streets on Saturday evenings, and the visitation of public-houses. A solo The secret of a happy day "was given by Miss Mason. On the sug- gestion of the Rev E Morgan the audience testified their appreciation of the services of chairman and lecturer by a hearty round of applause.

ST. HELENS.

"THE GOLDEN LAND OF FAIRY TALES."

PRESENTATION TO SIR JOSEPH BEECHAM.

The memorable week of the St. Helens Children's Festival—" The Golden Land of Fairy Tales" ended on Saturday night, amid remarkable scenes. At the conclusion of the "Golden Vision" the Mayor (Sir Joseph Beechnut) appeared in response to a general call from the audience in the theatre, and little Master Harold Barrett (The Hare) handed to him two small gold watohchain charms—a dog and a hare—in a case which bore the inscription : Presented to the Mayor of St. llelens, Alder- man Sir Joseph Beecham, by the theatre's general manager, A. M. Loader, es a memento of the most unique event in the history of the St. Helens Theatre, The Golden Land of Fairy Tales,' played by invitation of the Mayor to 12,000 school children of the Borough of St. Helens, February itil2. 'Let us all ring Fancy's knell ; I'll begin it: Ding, dong, bell.'" Sir Joseph, in acknowledging the cheers of the audience and members of the company, said that when , he conceived the idea of bringing "fee Golden Land of Fairy Tales "to St. Helens he only had the children in mind. But it occurred to him afterwards that the children of older growth would like to see the plays. It had been no small undertaking to have brought everything from London between Saturday and Monday and their stage manager was to be congratulated on such an achievement. He had no doubt that as the play had proceeded they bad all been reminded of the days of youth and of the fairy tales they learned at their mothers' knees: and, if so, they could quite under- stand the impression that had been created upon !he children during the week. They had had over 12,000 children present, and their attention had been riyetted on the stories and their enthusiasm had been very great. The sight they presented and the way they took the whole matter had greatly affected all his friends who bad been there. It had, indeed,

been a week of very great pleasure. He thanked every% member of the company, of the orchestra, the staff, and everybody concerned for the willing way they had worked to make the children's festival a success. (Applause.) lie felt sure that when they went back to London they would look back upon their visit to St. Helens with pleasure and the satisfaction of knowing that through their efforts the children of the town had been made happy end brought into the " golden land of fairy tales." At the invitation of Sir Joseph all present then rose and sang the National Anthem. Mr. Albert Archdeacon, the general man seer of the company, said that what they had done was absolutely unique in the theatrical world.

ST. HELENS AND THE COAL CRISIS. NOTICES POSTED AT ST. HELENS GLASSWORKS.

Great surprise and dismay were created in trading circles at St. Helens on Monday by the announcement that Messrs. Pilkington Bros, had during the afternoon given notice to every department of their three huge works terminating the engagements of all their employees on the 2nd March. The glassworks provide the bulk of the employment in the town, and certainly more than half the population are dependent on them for their daily bread. The notices were Fosted at the plateglass works, Cowley-hill, in all sections of the sheet and roll plate works, at the Ravenbead Plate-glass Works, and also at the timber and boxmaking works. Between ten and twelve thousand men, boys, and women are employed in these works, and as it is rumoured that similar notices are being issued at some of the local glass bottle works, the outlook is the most serious that St. Helens people ever bad to contemplate. The firm are owners of two large collieries where over 2,000 men are employed, and the coal strike, if it is unhappily entered upon, will mean the complete paralysis of the trade of the town. The position cannot be viewed with anything but the gravest anxiety, and if similar action is taken by the employers in other Lancashire towns the County Palatine will soon be thrown into an unprecedented condition of chaos. The following are the terms of the notice posted throughout the glassworks, jware- houses, &c. : " Notice.—Tn the event of a coal strike it will be impossibia to maintain supplies of fuel for the furnaces: and the works will have to be closed down to a large extent. In consequence the firm regret that they will be unable to provide their glassroakers with full employ- ment and all their other workmen. They are reluctantly obliged to give notice to terminate their engasements on March 2nd. Such work as can be done will be dis- tributed amongst the greatest number possible.— (Signed) Pilkington Brothers, Limited."

The Infirmary Million Penny Fund.

Sir,—lt will no doubt be of interest to your readers to know the position of the Million Penny Fund,—At a meeting of the Committee on February 19th the secretary (Mr. H. Collin) submitted a statement of the accounts to that date. The amount of a million pennies is £1,166 13s. 4d , and towards this sum £3,385 2s. sd. has been actually received, and a further X. 275 promised but not paid. Expenses to date are £ll5 14s. 10d., so that if the Infirmary authorities are to receive a clear million pennies £622 ss. 9d. will still be required. The latest effort of the Committee in organising a whist drive and dance benefitted our funds to the extent of £5O, and I dosire to personally thank all those who worked to bring about such a successful result. A further meeting of the Committee will be held on Wednesday evening next at 8 o'clock at the Infirmary to consider a scheme for raising the balance of the money, and I should be glad of donations to enable the fund to be closed as early as possible and to encourage the Committee in the work they have so splendidly carried on.—Yours faithfully, JAMES HENRY SMETHURST

._ _ (Chaaman).

===== 0002601_19120224_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
Drunkenness in Warrington..

Sir,—On reading the letter by " Sobriety "in your issue of Saturday last, for which all friends of temperance are grateful, the question which occurs to me is "does the increase in arrests for drunkenness denote an increase in actual drunkenness " ? No statistics are more generally misleading than those referring to di unkenness. Is it tie fact that Warrington is more drunken or is it that the statistics merely prove more effective police supervision ? A slight slackening of vigilance on the part of the police and,so far as statistics are concerned,Warrington would cease to occupy the unenviable position which is hers to-day. While we know that drunkenness is so appallingly prevalent, I for my part vs ould welcome extra police activity even if the tesult were a still greater increase in arrest.,. So far as the police are trying to cope with this evil let us help them in enforcing the law as regards : (I) Hours of opening; (2) Permitting drunkenness cn licensed premises; (3) Serving mtcxicants to persons already drunk. Such a policy added to a vigorous and continuous propaganda campaign ought to do something to mini- mise the power of the drink trade even in Wart ington. If loyal citizens, Churchmen and otherwise, co-opetat- ing with the police and magistrates, do their part to diminish the drink evil, then indeed there would be need for rejoicing at a reduction in arrests for drunkenness, but at present I doubt whether "Statistics for drunkenness " ate a reliable indication as to an increase or otherwise of intemperance. The drinking habits of the people menace tt e workers at all paints. Diminished consumption of intoxicants means increased spending power in other commodities, and a benefit, from the wage-earner's point of view, far in excess of any displacement of labour caused by a reduction in the output of intoxi- cants.

When the wage-earners realise how closely their welfare, both in their home life and industrially, depends on their grappling with the drink traffic, then [trust we shall witness both a real and a statistical reduction in drunkenness. Excessive drinking is almost invariably the result of ignorance—Yours &c., THOMAS D. LESLIE. February 22nd, 1912.

Evelyn-street School Headmastership. The Case for Warrington Teachers. Sir,— I regret that a long standing engagement at Lymm compelled me to leave the Education Com- mittee's meeting on Monday last before I could find an opportunity of taking part in the discussion and division on the Evelyn-strcet Headmastership, but by your courtesy I should like to make a few comments upon the position. Mr. Kingdon, as representing the Chamber of Commerce, spoke strongly in favcur of throwing the position open to national competition, stating that it was the unanimous feeling cf the representatives of the firms constitutinr, the Chamber tnat this should be done. I did not gather that this opinion was the result of serious discussion and resolution in the Chamber, or whether it was the irresponsible chatter of the smoke-room ; but, assuming that it was the fames, I venture to say that such an opinion coming from the firms composing the Chamber is nothing less than amazing. Surely the tongues of the representa- tives must have been unduly protuberant in theircheeks when such an opinion was expressed. It needs no demonstration to show that good positions inmany of the firms in Warrington and elsewhere are never thrown open to competition.lt is nepotism not merit,wito a ni4n is, not what he is, that determines the appointment in the great majority of cases. It is notorious that in many firms tke avenues to promotion at e blocked by the sons, sons-in-law,nephews, protises of proprietors, or directors, or those holding a considerable financial interest in the concern. And these virtuous gentle- men of the Chamber of Commerce from the pedestal of their lofty superiority, lay down the law for the appointment of a headmaster :—We must have the "best " man. Does Mr. Parker seriously contend that in the appointment of managers of tanneries, or any other indunti ml concern, the position is always thrown open. to national competition through advertisement? Is it not far more frequently the case that men are pro- moted to these positions from the lower grade of service, because of the knowledge the Directors have gained of them while under their observation and in their employ? But to promote men already in the employ of the Education Committee is not " businesslike.'" It is admitted, of course, that the cream of businessmen are to be found in Warrington, but comparatively insignificant places like London, Birmingham, Sheffield, Leeds Manchester, Liverpool, must have some men of ordinary business capacity in their civic government, must have some men of knowledge and experience in educational administration, especially seeing that these places have had School Boards and Education Cr mmittees for more than forty years, and yet these authorities never advertise their headmaster- ships but promote their assistants. But, Sir, we are all agreed upon the necessity of appointing the " best " man, and I maintain that there are already in the employ r f the committee a Con- siderable number of " best " men. There are, I believe, about eighty certificated male teachers in Warrington, every one of whom is qualified by his certificate to take charge of :this school and against whose appointment on the ground of qualification no exception could be taken. No one will deny that amongst these there must be some who are unsuitable Pr the position. (That unsuitability has been discovered by local knowledge. You have no such knowledge of the outeider.) Amongst the Warrington male teachers there are men who bold brilliant University degrees, who have hosts of certificates in science, music, art, and manual work, who have had charge of Continua- tion Schools which by their record and success have excited the envy of neighbouring authorities, who have long yeare of strenuous and successful service behind them, and whose lives and records axe known to their fellow townsmen. Why then, it is asked, do the Wart ington teachers fear open competition?ln the first place there is no such thing as open competition. The Warrington teachers are rigidly barred from appointment to the headmastersh i pa of other local authorities. In 1007 a return was made showing that 91 authorities restricted appointments to teachers in their employment and 31 other authorities gave preference to their own teachers. During the past fortnight I have received replies respecting the practice of more than 100 authorities and the great majority restrict the appointments to their own employees. The exceptions are as a rule small authorities, or County Authorities having only small schools. . . .

There are also the vagaries of appointing Committees to be feared. It has sometimes happened that even

===== 0002601_19120224_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, February, 24, 1912.

on academic grounds the best man has not been appointed. Long service is sometimes overlooked because the exceptional personal appearance of a candidate has captivated the members of a Committee. The wider you make the field of selection, the chance of the Warrington teacher becomes more slender. The Warrington teachers ask only to be put on the same footing in regard to promotion as their profes- sional brethren in the majority of other towns. 'that request I believe will be considered reasonable by the great majority of Warrington citizens.—Yours truly, JOHN UPSON.

Dumping in France.

How Tariffs Make the Consumer Pay.

Sir,—l see from references made in recent issues of the " Examiner " that you have lately had a' Dump- ing Exhibition" in Warrington. From the display in tradesmen's windows in this part of France there is a Dumping Exhibition of English firm's goods all the year round, the following amongst many ethers always being in great prominence : Keiller's Jams and Marmalade. Huntley and Palmer's Biscuits. Crosee and Blackwell's and Lazenby's Pickles. Pottea Meats, etc. _ .

Lipton's and Horniman's Teas. Sunlight, Vinolia (which I think is Crcsfield's) an Pear's Soaps. Colman's Mustard. B urroughs and Wellcome and Co. Tabloids an Powders.

bingers Sewing Machines are almost the only ones seen. Even the road steam roller is English make and the fire engine and equipment by Merryweathers, London. The public lavatories are by Millar, Liver- pool. There are also quantities of linen, woollen and cotton goods too nnmerous to mention, but these he it understood not at prices paid in England, which lot oyes the absurdity of the statement that the exporter pays the tax. Since corning here 1 have often wished he did, the cost of everything being much higher than at home as you will see from the following figures taken ft om my wife's housekeeping book :

Beef steak, Is 8d to is 10d per lb. Pork, Is 8d to :Is Gd per lb. Tinned tongue, 3s 4d per lb. Plain biscuits, Is 3d to is Gd such as sold at home for 8d to 10d.

Keiller's jam and marmalade, 10d per lb. jar. Colman's mustard,4d per 2uz tin. Coffee 2s Gd per lb ',is Sd quality in England). Sunlight soap 4d per lb. Quaker oats Is 8d per 21b packet. Common salt 2d per lb. Old potatoes 2d per lb. new 4d. 21b loaf, common quality, id. Lump sugar 5d per lb. Wax candles Is per lb. Paraffin oil 51d per quart. Tea 3s 9d per lb (2s quality in England). 1 also pay 10d per oz for Willa' Capstan Nary Cut tobacco, and matcbes are so dear that the poor still resort to flint and steel and cotton wick.

Should any of your readers doubt the accuracy of these figures I shall be happy to supply the tradesmen's names to enable them to be verified.

I should like to have shown how farm produce brought into the town from outside villages is also made dear by octroi, or local taxes. set up to protect each little community from its neighbours, but tear I have already trespassed too far on your valuable space. - Yours &c., . .

ALFRED RADFIRTEL

82, Rue Gambetta, St. Jean de Luz, France.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

From the overture to the National Anthem there is not one dull moment at the Hippodrome this week. Now, when the vaudeville theatre has attained such a high degree of excellence: and there is such a call lon artistes it is rarely that one does not find at least one turn which does not meet with the approvel of the all too critical audiences. However, every turn at the Hippodrome this week is of such a high quality that even the severest critic cannot fail to appreciate. The pick of a good bunch of attractions is undoubtedly "Fitz," the famous boxing kangaroo. He "shows off" his marvellous knowledge of the pugilistic art ins short encounter with his trainer, Professor Tully, to the delight and satisfaction of the audience. He is a tricky opponent and does not strictly adhere to the set rules. The hind leg " punch " is one of his favourites and would no doubt stand him in good stead in a " rough and tumble "about.Wilf I3unaand appears with great success in character studies of comedy and dramatic artistes. His impersonations of Jack Pleasants, George Formby, Barry Brown and Sir Henry Irving are indeed realistic and his " make up" is faultless. A comedy duologue" Our Wedding Anniversary" by Mr. and Mrs. W, J. Manning is a bilge laughter provoker. whilst John and Lilian Grand, operatic vocalists and character actors charm the audience with their vocal production "At home and abroad." A clever acrobatic turn is that provided by Ricirdo and Salving, and Sam Osborne earns a fair share of applause with his Scotch gags and songs. Olive Plant is a pleasirg comedienne her rendition of " Just someone " being exceptionally good. The six Donovans provide a neat musical entertainment, their manipulation of several instruments being indeed praiseworthy. In addition to such a fine array of variety artistes a humorous picture " Muscle development " concludes a most enjoyable entertainment.

NATIONAL INSURANCE. PROGRESSIVE LABOUR LEAGUE

MEETING.

The Tory Campaign of misrepresentation.

The Warrington Progressive Labour League is conducting a vigorous campign in support of the National Insurance Act, On Monday, the second of a series of meetings, at which the provisions of the measure are carefully explained, was held in tbe National School, Church-street. Mr. M. Llewellyn, who presided, was supported by Mr. T. U. Garside (Manchester), Mr. L. Wynne Jones and Mr. J. J. Morton.—There was a good attendance. The CHAIRMAN, in opening the proceedings, said he was glad ti.e Progressive Labour League ha,.. come for- ward to make known to the electors the inner workings of the Insurance Act. Ile was also glad that they were living in an age when it was not quite necessary for him to apologise for being a chairman and " only a workingman. (Hear, hear.) The Bill affected them as workingmen ; they stood to gain everything by it. If there was anything in it about which they had their doubts, then it was their business td inquire, iii their own interests, and see exactly how they etood. They had recently had a

DU:gPING RXHIBITION

in Warrington, and in that was a bill which ran : The onlp way to get ninepence for fourpence is by Tariff Reform" (Laughter) lie was glad Mr. Boma Law had been trapped into using the word "Certainly ' so emphatically in revonse to the Prime Minister's question as to whether he would repeal the Insurance Act. They now knew what to expect if the Tory party got into power, The sick, maternif y, sanatoria and other benefits would all be in jeopardy and it was very evident, and on the top of that they would have their foods taxed. (Floor, hear.) Ile was glad workingmen were beginning to appreciate things for themselves. They were not going to he persuaded by the public house landlord who explained an act or a measure to his own liking. To-day men were getting more consideration than ever

18 for his 41 would belong to the mail of 40 for the same amount. There was no friendly society that could come within miles of that. How could it be done. They would see in all large towns big buildings for the Insurance Companies. The members had paid for those. When a man could not pay hi a contributions his money was swelled up and went to someone else. For every 44. a man stopped paying to a society that society was £3 lOs better off. Round the neck of every society there was a chain of liability to which a man who paid his fourpnce was paying. Every additional fourpence paid increased that chain by exactly the amount of insurance the fourpence would buy. There was less expectancy of sickness in a youth at 16 than in a man at 40, and therefore the liability on a man at 40 was greater. The Act said they were all 16 andail p, ,d 4d Now the people's money that had been saved was going tothe Government. The people alone could rbb themselves of the money because no one else could use it or benefit by it. Every solitary copper of the funds was for the people to use. If a man was a member of two friendly societies and paying IS. per week he could not afford to pay an extra fourpence. They could say to the secretary of one of the societies "I want fourpence to go through you to the State." Then the man got all the benefits that the other twopence would buy and for the other society to which he was paying sixpence he got all the benefits be received before and the additional benefits that could be got by the money set free from liability. They wanted to abolish the word charity from the dictionary—(hear, hear)—and let the money come to the people in the shape of wages they bad earned. The Act was also for the prevention of sickness and would a long way towards the abolition of slum dwellings. The people would have a voice in what was to be done for the people. (Hear, bear.) The Act was the second instalment of that charter of democratic liberty that working men said they had been wanting for over 100 years. l'Applause). DIR. GRAHAM GILMOUR KILLED.

Mr. Graham Gilmour, one of the best- known aviators in Great Britain, was killed on Saturday while flying a new monoplane. He left Brooklands, in a Martin Handsyde machine, with the intention of taking a short pleasure trip. When over Richmond the

monoplane collapsed and crashed to the ground. He fell a distance of between 300 and 400 feet, death being instantaneous. Our picture allows the wrecked machine and a portrait of Mr. Gilmour,

ever introduced. When the Act was passing through the House of Commons, Mr. Boner Law, who had so emphatically said " Certainly " to the question which wns put to him by Mr. Asquith, had neither said "Yes" nor ' No." The Tories of to-day would go down to posterity as the" Yes-No" Party. (Laughter.) If they bad not wanted the Bill they should have taken their courage in both hands and voted against the measure. He thought the time would come when they would DO longer be able to misrepresent the Act. It would speak for itself. (Hear, hear.) They would wonder however they got along without it. If it was not for the work done by Friendly Society's in the past the National Insurance scheme would not have been possible. They were going to bring good medical attendance within the reach of everybody. He hoped no one in that room would take alarm at what the doctors were doing at the present time. Some of the doctors (that was consulting doctors) were more concerned with a long- lasting quarrel with the British Medical Association. They were using this opportunity to have a dig at that Association. There was not a word in the Act which stated how much they were going to be paid. If there was going to be any hardship in the working of the Act the Government would accept amendments. They should not attempt to destroy the Act as they were doing. (Hear, hear.) He believed the doctor stood to gain—looking at the question from its hughest and noblest standpoint. lie anew that when the Act got into proper working order everyone would welcome it. It would brighten the homes and do a great deal for the social amelionition of the people of this country. (Applause). He proposed a vote of thanks to the speaker. Mr. J. J. Mourox, in seconoing, said that they were doing right in having the Act explained. It was out of all harmony with other Acts, and the people could hardly realise without explanation what benefits they would derive. (Applause), The vote was heartily passed.

MR. lIAROLD AND REV. E. C. E.

'BA:ciLETON CRITICISED.

There was a good attendance at a meeting to explain the provisions of the National Insurance Act which was held in the Hamilton-street Schools on Wednesday evening, Mr P Peacock presided and he was supported by Councillor Grindle, of Eccles, and Mr E G llughes. The CHAIRMAN in opening the proceedings said at the outset he would like to explain, in 0155 Mr Harold Smith heard they were haviog that meeting, that it had not been paid for out of the taxes they were collecting from the country. (Laughter.) The meeting had been convened in order to explain the advantages and benefits which all the workers in the country would obtain through the great Liberal National Insurance Act. It bad been found necessary to send out mitsionaries to explain the real meaning of the Act. The reason was, that the Tory party who were so nice at the first and second readings of the Act bad sent out their minions to falsely repre- sent the Bill in order to win one or two bye-elections. They, the Liberals, had got experts down in order to convince the people that it was an Act that had been , put forward for the benefit of the workingclass of the country. Mr. Harold Smith was at his best when he was "mud slinging." He came from that class which suited Liverpool, Birkenhead and some Warrington audiences when he was vilifying his opponents. They never found that in Arthur Crosfield. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Smith had said "that Lloyd George did not only disgrace the high office he held as Minister but that Lloyd George studied his personal advantages first." After- wards he considered party advantages and also put class against class and employees against employers." And the Tory audience swallowed all his speech about Lloyd George as If that was all the eminent Minister was fit for. They had benefitted through the great help of Lloyd George in the Liberal Govern- ment. Mr. Harold Smith suggested that Lloyd George " should look after the interests of this great Empire." Ile,(the speaker) thought he was doing what every great Minister ought to do in looking after the greater interssts of the people. (Hear, hear.) In regard to the land taxes Mr. Smith had told them that Mr. Lloyd George had done something very much wrong.Mr Lloyd George had explained that question before the public and Parliament. (Hear, hear.) He had proved his case and that Mr. Harold Smith was wrong. The speaker went on to illustrate the chief advantages offered. by the Insurance Act and said the friendly societies would have been ill-advised if they bad rejected the Bill. Two-thirds of the friendly societies in the country were insolvent. They would be made sound and profitable under the Act. If a man wanted to transfer to another society he could do so without forfeiting the money he had paid in to the society he wanted to leave, which had not been the case prior to the Act. It was the same with works clubs and if another society would not take him from a works club he could compel the latter to keep him in. They could join as many societies as they liked but could only draw national benefits from the one which was an approved society. Then there were the welcome maternity benefits. Those alone would make a great difference to future generations. (Hear, hear.) Continuing Mr. Peacock said they all knew even in Warrington that there was a big drain on the friendly societies for consnmptive brethren. The friendly societies and everyone would welcome the arrangements in the Act for those who were sufferers from that complaint. The doctors had said they would not be under the thumb of friendly society members. They friendly society members knew in Warrington

that when there was a vacancy for a doctor, members of the profession asked those whom they thought had any influence to do their best to get them the job. But why this trouble? The doctor would get more. money under the Act than he got to-day. He only got about 3s. 6d. or 41. in War- rington from friendly societies, and attended members and gave them reedicine.Nlr Lloyd George had estimated the payment at 63. The doctors wanted about 103 and then they might be satisfied, but at the expense of the workers. They were going to get 50 per cent. more under the Act than now. It would be a bad day's work for them if they did not consider the interests of the people of the country instead of their own selflah ends. (Hear, hear.) He had read a letter in a news- paper from the Rev EC E Carleton. His advice to the rev gentleman was that he ought to stick to his work and do what he was paid to do instead of striking at everything the Liberal Party introduced. He had said in his letter " Fanc7 subordinating the health of the people to the financial schemes of this vain self-willed minister— Lloyd George." Then again Medical service is of more importance to the community then any scheme of insurance that ever existed." He would suggest to the rev, gentleman that if be would not mix up religion and politics, his parish and the workers at large would be the better.—ln conclnsion, the Chairman said the Act would benefit employer as well as employee. The employer would get increased efficiency. So long as the policy of the Tory party was to benefit only one out of a hundred he was not with them. The result of the Act would be a happy and contented nation. (Applause). Councillor GRINDLE, in a convincing address on the Act, said that Act was sure to "take on" because throughout the country it was creating intense interest, and only needed to be understood to be accepted. Mr. E. G Huotrks, in proposing a vote of thanks to the speaker, said that if the dootors could make a profit out of 45., they were bound to make more ont of 63 (Applause).
