they had done before. (Hear, hear.) After the Insurance Act had been in force be Vat 3 sure it would need no platfurin advocacy as its beneficent results would be its own and the best advocacy. (Hear, hear and applause). Mr. GARsIDE, who was accorded a hearty reception said there were two questions which suggested t,hem-, selves with regard to the measure. One was " a Insurance Bill at all" ? and the other was "Is the method adopted the best ? " For his, (the speaket's) part in the very many meetings he had addressed on the Bill he had always declined to consider it or say one word about it from a party point of view. It was not a party measure. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Lloyd George was the first man in the House of Commons to admit the tremendous benefit he had derived from the help and suggestions of the Opposition. The principle under- lying the Bill on which tbe details had been worked oot was distinctly non-party from their point of view. Whatever he would say about the Act was from a non- party stand-point and he wanted to have nothing to do with any discussion except from a financial and economic standpoint. (Hear. hear). -WHY THE BILL AT ALL? There were 19,000,000 of people in the country who had to earn their living more or less by the labour of their hands. There wen probably about 3,000,000 who did not depend entirely on their hands for a livelihood but the balance depended entirely and absolutely on manual labour for a living. (Hear, hear). Probably the strongest fact, and certainly the most material evidence as to the necessity of National Insurance was to be obtained from those figures, because after over 100 years of friendly society work they had not ' more than 5,000,000 members of friendly societies to-day. There were more men and women in the country who had been members of friendly societies, and were not now, than there were at present members. That was to say, if they had 5,C00,000 members to-day there were more than that number who had been, but were not now members. How was it that more men did not join the friendly societies ? and again, how was it that two-thirds of the friendly societies now existing bad a great struggle to pay their way ? The fact was, that there were only 155 societies out of nearly 500 that could pay their way. How was it that they had so few members ? The old men were staying in, but they could not get the young ones to join, . The old men had greater liability to sickness than the young men. How was it they could not get those with less liability to share the greater liability of the old ? Why did they turn their backs on friendly societies ? The purchasing power of 20s. was not as much now as formerly. House rents ud rates had gone up. The price of a good suit of clothes had gone up ; cheap articles they could buy at any street corner, mostly German ; dumped if you like. (Laughter.) The purchasing power of money had, however, gone down and that necessitated an examination as to the people themselves. They found the people divided into two classes. There were men whom they could not persuade to make any provision for themselves at all. Had there not possibly been men at. the cup-tie that after- noon who bad spent their sixpences and shillings vvbo could not afford them ? Would there not probably be scores of men who would go to the cup final whose wives and families would have "to pay the piper " for that trip ? There were a great and increasing number of men who were losing their sense of responsi- bility. The "DON'T CARE "SPIRIT.

was coming in, and if sickness came, it did not matter so long as a neighbour next door saw that they did not want a fire or a loaf. There was another thing true. There were men who could not make any provision because their wages would not allow it. What were they going to do with those people; the men who oould not and the men who would not. Not only the Liberal Government but the Conservatives had recognised that something should be done. The question was how to face it ? The Act said to those who felt they could not make adequate provision, "We will help you " and to the man who declined to make any provision "We will make you." (Hear, hear and applause). Then the other question arose " How was it to be done ?" Various suggestions bad been made in regard to the payments. He would admit that there was much in the measure that needed altering, and many things that might be made better than they *ere. They must put it into operation and gain by experience. (Hear, hear and applause.) The Bill did not propose to destroy any societies, but it said to them that they could not run their societies as they wanted to do. The Act said it would take them over and enable them to run the societies in a proper manner. He would just take the normal figures and show them how it was going to be done. 'rake the fourpence. The Act said the individual could only come into the Act through the Society. Takes man at 30 years of age. The Act said " You pay 4d., the employer pays 3d. and the 'State adds another 2d." Now said the other side—Mr. Harold Smith wifh a grin said "There you are, you are going to give the working man ninepence for fourpence." They would not say that, they said they were going to give him for his fourpence just as much as the friendly society could give him for ten times fourpence. In friendly societies , the rates and benefits differed according to the ages of ' members at the time they joined. If a man was a member no matter what his age whether from 16 to 40, the Act raid the' were all 16 and had to pay only 4d and it provided lOs per benefit for all of them. The various benefits that would go to the young man of j

NATIONAL SERVICE LEAGUE.

' The Ideals of a Democratic State. There was a pleasant convivial gathering in the dining hall of Messrs. J. Crosfleld and Sons, Ltd„ on Tuesday when the annual dinner of the Warrington Division of the National Service League was held. Captain G R Crosfield presided and amongst others present were Mr and Mrs B S Townroe, Mr Mate Reid (Manchester), Mr Plinston, Mr H M Ashton, Mr T Richardson, and Mr E Ball. Mr. SLATE B Emsecretary to the Manchester Divisi on of the League, in proposing the health of " Mr. T. H. Lyon, the president of the Warrington Division," said this was pre-eminently a time for some degree of self- congratulation. They had, as their Chairman would bear him out, every reason in Lancashire to be satisfied with the work the Lancas'iire division of the League had done, but while they felt they were justified in expressing some satisfaction, yet it was not in the nature of Lancashire men to allow self-congratulation to carry them so far as to make them oblivious of what faults might have existed in their organisations. (Hear, hear.) Many objections hid been raised against the scheme at various meetings and one of them was that the scheme was undemocratic in tone and conception. That raised the question as to what was meant by the idea of democracy. Mr Jeremy Bentham's ices of democracy was to secure the greatest happiness for the greatest number. They could not find a betner definition than that. That being the conception of democracy they had to view it in the light not only of the privileges but also of the risponsibilities which such a conception of government entailed. He was sure they would agree with him that the side which concerned their responsibility as members of a great democratic State was even more important than the side which concerned the benefits from such a scheme of government. (Hear, hear.) There was no greater necessity for devotion to the State than in the realm of the practical defence of that State against its enemies both within and without the Commonwealth. Everyone would agree that it. was the plain duty of everybody to guard the State against the onslaught and iocursions of coveters from within and enemies from without. (Hear, hear.) Having shown that national service was looked upon as an obligation in Anglo- Saxon times, the speaker said it was the plain duty of everybody to defend the State against onslaught both from within and without. There were those who were perfectly willing to see the reasonableness of the defence of the State from within, but who had the utmost difficulty in being persuaded to see the other side. The true ideal of all democratic Constitution was that the individual citizen should be responsible for the defence of his own home. (Applause.) The League advocated the true democracy, because not only were they advocating a participation in the ideals and pleasures of the people, but also their share in the responsibilities of democracy. (Applause). PRIZE DISTRIBU fl ON. -

_ Mrs Townroe then presented the prizes to the successful competitors as follows : Open Competition.—Mr Plinston scored 32 in this competition, but "stood down "and second and third fired off. Mr Lyon's Challenge Cup.—Sergeant Instructor J Allardice and Mr J Sankey tied and fired off, 31.; Mr F 0 Bennion, 31; Mr F Garside, 30 ; Mr E Pollard, 30; Mr H Bennett, 30 ; Mr H Potts, sem. 30 ; Mr Shepherd, 28.

Old Soldiers' Competition.—Colonel Reid Walker Challenge Cup : Mr .1 W Plinston, 33; Mr A Adams, 30 ; Mr J W Elliott, :10 i Sergeant J Lloyd, 29. Civilian Competition.—Mr C P Day. 32 ; Mr J Hartley, 32 ; Mr T 3 Growoott, 31:e1r d Potts, jun.,29. Mr. 11. S. TowNROE in proposing a vote of thanks to the Shooting Committee, the artistes and the donors of the prizes said no-one knew the amount of work performed and the trouble the shooting committee took to ensure good marksmanship for the members in War- rington. He believed the members derived great benefit from the shooting provided. The Territorial organisa• tima was a great step forward in defensive work. In Warrington the Territorial Battalion was not up to strength, and those who were capable of rendering service should see that the numbers reached establish- ment strength. They were proud of the fact that Warrington was one of the first places to have a strong branch of the League. They were, however, ashamed of the fact that they still needed recruits in the 4th South Lancashire Regiment. On behalf of Captain Crosfteld and himself he appealed to the members of the League to help them to raise the battalion to full strength by the time they went to camp next August. Every man in the Terri- torial Force was a stepping stone to National Service and the more men they had in the Force the more men they would have convinced of the necessity for com- pulsory military training. They would not be satisfied even if the Force came up to strength, because the men wanted more training. (Applause). Mr. BALL, in response, urged each member to get not only a Territorial for the South Lancashire Regiment but to use all his influence to secure incremed membership for the league. (Applause). Mr. T. RICHARDSON, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Chairman, said it needed no words of his to emphasise what they thought of Captain Crosfield. They all knew his value to the League, the work of which depeoded upon him. They might have faults.but Captain Crosfleld very soon put them right. (Applause.) Lie also coupled with the vote of thanks their friend Mrs. Townroe, for the admirable manner in which she bad presented the prizes. (Applause). Captain CIZOSLIELD, in responding, said he did not wish to introduce any sad note, but at, the same time those who really took a serious view of international politics must confess that the future did not look too bright. We should he all right if we were properly prepared, but all the Norman Angelis in the world were not going to stop war if one nation was weak and another I happened to be very powerful and bad a war party at I the head of affairs. He had been to Germany and had the greatest admiration for the Germans, who, he thought, as a people, did not want war. In every nation, however, they had a war party and undonbtodly there was one in Germany, and if an occasion arose when tbey thought they bad us napping and our young men could not defend the country they might " have a go at us." War in this country would be a tern i ble thing and be prayed God it might never happen. The commonsense view was for our young men to be pre- pared to defend their own shores so that nothing could happen. That was the view of the National Service League and people were gradually adopting that view ; also realizing that quite apart from the question of defence three or four months' training at eighteen or nineteen years of age would be a godsend to the men of the c mntry, They in order to bring about the great reform that Earl Itoberts had proposed to thena nation should (l a A p p al us::: .k hard r A capital vocal and entertaining programme was gone through during the evening, the artistes being Miss Garnett Ball, Mr H Bennett, Mr Ashton, Mr E Walker, Mr A Bennett, Mr Princet and Mr Townroe.

LOCAL WEDDING.

Gasby—Broadhurst. A. pretty wedding tok place at the Gospel Hall, Forster-street, on Tuesday, when Colour-Sergeant Samuel Gasby, of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment, son of the late Mr. Gasby, of Liverpool, was married to Miss Jessie Broadhurst, dauahter of Mr. George Broadhurst, Brick-street, Warrington. The service was conducted in the presence of a large con- gregation by Mr. F. Broadhurst, of Waterloo (uncle of the bride) and Mr. T. Holt of Warrington. Mr. W. Street was at the organ and played Mendelssohn's Wedding March and cther appropriate nausic. The bride, who was g.ven away by her father, was beautifully attired in cream voile, with wreath and veil, and carried a bouquet of tulips and lilies, with streamers. The bridesmaids were the Misses Marie and Maggie Broadhurst (sisters of the bride). They wore pretty lavender grey dresses, with black picture hats, and their bouquets were composed of tulips and lilies. Their ornaments were gold chains and lockets, the gifts of the bridegroom. Sergeant Earle acted as best man. After the ceremony a reception was held at the home of the bride's parents, after which the happy couple departed for London. The bride travelled in a reseda green dress, with black picture hat. Many handsome presents were received.

M-E,O 0.0.M1 *EitiralKlEE 0. 00.41 PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

immerse the bead of any stricken ones Into it. A few applications generally prove sufficient. Always remember to isolate ailing chickens from the rest of the stock until quite well. Any that die should be burnt. COOPS. At different ages you are forced to transfer the chicks into larger coops. Always see that the coops are well lime-washed before doing so. Coops that are kept well lime-washed and clean are a good pre- ventive against disease, providing always that you do not overcrowd. This is a very common error, some- times made with the very best intention, that is to assist in keeping them warm. If the coops are draught-proof you need have little fear but that they will be quite warm enough. More chickens die. through overcrowding and bad housing than from the effects of cold.
